=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is an H \times W grid. Let (h,w) denote the cell at the h-th row from the top and the w-th column from the left. A non-negative integer A_{h,w} is written in cell (h,w).
Takahashi starts at cell (sh,sw) and will perform Q changes to the grid. The i-th change is given by a character d_i (d_i is one of L, R, U, D) and a non-negative integer a_i, meaning Takahashi will do the following:

- Move one cell in the direction d_i. That is, if d_i is L, move left; if R, move right; if U, move up; if D, move down by one cell. Then, let the destination cell be (h,w), and set A_{h,w} to a_i.

It is guaranteed that in each change, he can move one cell in direction d_i.
After each change, print the answer to the following problem:

A sequence of cells P = ((h_1,w_1), \ldots, (h_{M},w_{M})) is said to be a path if and only if it satisfies all of the following conditions:

- (h_1,w_1) = (1,1), (h_{M},w_{M}) = (H,W), and M = H + W - 1.
- For every i with 1 \leq i \leq M-1, either (h_{i+1}, w_{i+1}) = (h_i + 1, w_i) or (h_{i+1}, w_{i+1}) = (h_i, w_i + 1).

There are \binom{H+W-2}{H-1} paths. For a path P = ((h_1,w_1), \ldots, (h_{M},w_{M})), define f(P) = \prod_{1\leq i\leq M}A_{h_i,w_i}. Print the sum, modulo 998244353, of f(P) over all paths P.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W
A_{1,1} \cdots A_{1,W}
\vdots
A_{H,1} \cdots A_{H,W}
Q sh sw
d_1 a_1
\vdots
d_Q a_Q

Output

Print Q lines.
The i-th line should contain the sum, modulo 998244353, of f(P) over all paths P after performing the i-th change to the grid.

Constraints


- 2 \leq H, W \leq 200000
- HW \leq 200000
- 0 \leq A_{h,w} < 998244353
- 1 \leq Q \leq 200000
- 1 \leq sh \leq H, 1 \leq sw \leq W
- 0 \leq a_i < 998244353
- H, W, A_{h,w}, Q, sh, sw, and a_i are integers.
- Each d_i is L, R, U, or D.
- In each change, Takahashi can move one cell in the direction d_i.

Sample Input 1

2 3
1 2 3
4 5 6
3 2 2
U 7
R 8
L 9

Sample Output 1

456
666
822


- Initially, Takahashi is at (2,2).
- Move up, then set A_{1,2} to 7. The value of f(P) for each path is:
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 7 \times 3 \times 6=126.
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 7 \times 5 \times 6=210.
- P=((1,1),(2,1),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 4 \times 5 \times 6=120.


- Move right, then set A_{1,3} to 8. The value of f(P) for each path is:
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 7 \times 8 \times 6=336.
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 7 \times 5 \times 6=210.
- P=((1,1),(2,1),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 4 \times 5 \times 6=120.


- Move left, then set A_{1,2} to 9. The value of f(P) for each path is:
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 9 \times 8 \times 6=432.
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 9 \times 5 \times 6=270.
- P=((1,1),(2,1),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 4 \times 5 \times 6=120.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Alternative define U_v[x] = sum over paths from v to x of product excluding v but including x (R_v). Then T_v[x] = U_v[x] / A0[x] if A0[x]!=0, but zero issue. We can compute T_v directly with recurrence:
For x != v:
T_v[x] = (if up neighbor exists) (T_v[up] * A0[up]? if up != v else 1?) + (if left neighbor exists) similarly.
More generally, a path from v to x excluding v and x: let n be predecessor. The product of cells on path excluding v and x equals:
- If n == v: empty product = 1 (since v excluded, x excluded).
- If n != v: product excluding v and x = (product excluding v and n) * A0[n] (include n) = T_v[n] * A0[n].
So recurrence:
T_v[x] = (if up exists) (A0[up] * T_v[up] if up != v else 1) + (if left exists) (A0[left] * T_v[left] if left != v else 1).
This works with zeros. For x=v, T_v[v]=0 (or not used). For adjacent right, left neighbor = v, contribution 1, T=1.

But maybe we can compute T(v,u) for all dirty pairs using a single DP over the grid with multiple sources and "delta" weights? The recurrence for H[u] is exactly a DP over the grid with sources at dirty cells? Let's see.

We want solve H[u] for u in S:
H[u] = E0[u] + ∑_{v∈S, v<u} delta[v] H[v] T(v,u).

This is equivalent to defining a function X[x] for all cells:

However if we define source weights at dirty cells, the sum over v of delta[v] H[v] T(v,x) is the solution of a DP with sources at dirty cells. But if we process cells in topological order, when we reach a dirty cell u, X[u] can be computed from previous sources, then we can add a new source at u with strength delta[u]*X[u] to propagate to future cells. This is exactly like running DP over the entire grid once per block, maintaining an additional "correction field" Y[x] = ∑_{v∈S processed} delta[v] H[v] T(v,x). Let's formulate.

At block start, base grid. We want compute for every cell x:
H_cur[x] = E0[x] + Corr[x], where Corr[x] = sum over dirty v < x of delta[v] * H_cur[v] * T(v,x).
But H_cur[x] for non-dirty cells is not needed except to propagate? Actually Corr[x] can be computed by a DP over the grid:
For a fixed source at v with strength s_v = delta[v] * H_cur[v], its contribution to Corr[x] is s_v * T(v,x). T(v,x) satisfies the recurrence above. Therefore Corr[x] itself satisfies a linear recurrence with sources s_v at dirty cells. We can compute Corr[x] for all cells in topological order if we know s_v for dirty cells as we encounter them. But s_v depends on H_cur[v] = E0[v] + Corr[v], which is known when we reach v. So we can do a single pass over all cells in row-major order:
Initialize corr[1][1] = 0? For each cell (h,w) in topological order (h from 1..H, w from 1..W):
- corr[h][w] = contribution from sources before this cell. It can be computed from neighbors? Let's derive recurrence for corr.

Let corr[x] = ∑_{v∈S, v<x} s_v * T(v,x), where s_v = delta[v] * H_cur[v].
We need compute corr[x] from corr of predecessors plus sources? For a fixed source v, T(v,x) recurrence:
T(v,x) = (if up exists) (A0[up] * T(v,up) if up != v else 1) + (if left exists) (A0[left] * T(v,left) if left != v else 1).
Summing over sources v, with the condition if predecessor equals v, contribution is s_v * 1 (not s_v * A0[v]*T(v,v)). Since T(v,v)=0, but the "if predecessor == v" term is not captured by corr[predecessor]*A0[predecessor]. We need add source at v when moving from v to its down/right neighbors.

Let's define corr[x] for all cells as above. For x != start? For any x, corr[x] = sum over sources v < x s_v T(v,x). We can compute by:
corr[x] = (if up exists) [ A0[up] * corr[up] + (if up is dirty? s_up? wait source at up contributes to x via path up->x with T(up,x)=1, not A0[up]*T(up,up)? Let's use recurrence carefully.
For source v < x, let n be predecessor of x on path. If n == v, contribution to T(v,x) is 1 (empty product). If n != v, contribution is A0[n] * T(v,n). Thus:
T(v,x) = ∑_{n∈pred(x)} ( A0[n] * T(v,n) + [n==v] )
where pred(x) are up and left neighbors that exist. Here [n==v] is 1 if v is that neighbor, else 0. Note if v could be both up and left? Only if H=?? no, up and left distinct. So summing over sources:
corr[x] = ∑_{n∈pred(x)} [ A0[n] * corr[n] + ∑_{v∈S, v=n} s_v ]
= ∑_{n∈pred(x)} ( A0[n] * corr[n] + s_n )
where s_n = delta[n] * H_cur[n] if n is dirty, else 0.
This is a simple recurrence! Let's verify with one dirty u before v. Let corr at u? For source at u, corr[u] = 0 (since no source before u? Actually corr[u] may include sources before u). When computing corr at a neighbor x of u, pred includes u, so corr[x] gets A0[u]*corr[u] + s_u. If corr[u] includes previous sources, A0[u]*corr[u] accounts for previous sources' T(prev, u) * A0[u] as intermediate u, and s_u accounts for source at u with empty segment. This matches recurrence. Great!

Thus we can compute H_cur[x] = E0[x] + corr[x] for all cells in topological order, and when x is dirty, we know H_cur[x], compute s_x = delta[x] * H_cur[x], and use it in corr for down/right neighbors. We don't need precompute T pairs at all! This is huge.

Let's test with previous example: base start not dirty, dirty u before v. E0[v] base excluding v. corr[v] = from preds. Suppose source at u s_u = delta[u]*H_cur[u]. Wait if v is not adjacent to u, u is not a predecessor of v. The recurrence propagates step by step through all cells. At each cell, corr accumulates. For path u -> ... -> v, when moving from u to next cell, s_u added. Then at next cell y, corr[y] includes s_u*1. When moving y->z, corr[z] gets A0[y]*corr[y] (since corr[y] = s_u*T(u,y), and y intermediate, multiply by A0[y]) plus maybe s_y. This propagates. At v, corr[v] = s_u * T(u,v). Perfect.

At block start, base grid fixed. We have dirty set S (size B). The recurrence H[u] = E0[u] + ∑_{v∈S, v<u} delta[v] H[v] T(v,u). This is like a DP on a DAG with B nodes and edge weights T(v,u). Suppose we have current dirty set S and have computed H[u] for all u in S and answer. Now a new update at cell p changes its value from old current to new. If p was already dirty, just delta changes; if p new, add to S. We need update H for dirty cells downstream of p and answer. The recurrence is linear. Adding a source at p with strength s_p = delta[p] * H[p] (where H[p] is current excluding p after update) affects H[u] for u>p by s_p * T(p,u). But H[p] itself may change if p was already dirty? Let's handle.

If p is new dirty, before update p's value was base (or current? Actually if not dirty, current equals base). After update, delta_p = A1[p]-A0[p]. H[p] before update = E0[p] + corr_from_other_dirty[p] (current excluding p). This H[p] does not depend on A[p] (since excluding p), so it remains same after update. Then source strength s_p = delta_p * H[p]. It adds to downstream H[u] by s_p * T(p,u). So we can update H for all dirty u>p by adding s_p * T(p,u). That's O(B) if we can query T(p,u) O(1). Also answer update adds s_p * T(p,end) * A_end? Actually answer = A_end * H[end]; H[end] increases by s_p*T(p,end), so ans += A_end * s_p * T(p,end). If end is p? p cannot be end? It can; if updating end, A_end changes, answer scales? Need handle separately.

If p is already dirty, its delta changes from old_delta to new_delta (since A0 fixed, delta = current - A0). H[p] remains same? H[p] excludes p, so unaffected by A[p]. But H[p] may be affected by other dirty cells, not by p itself. So H[p] unchanged. The source strength s_p changes from old_delta*H[p] to new_delta*H[p]. We need adjust downstream H and answer by (new_s - old_s)*T(p,u). So O(B) per update. Great! This is promising.

Thus within a block, we can maintain:
- base grid A0, E0 (base prefix excluding cell).
- dirty set S with current delta = current_value - A0 for each dirty cell.
- Hcur[u] for each dirty cell u: current sum excluding u (under current grid).
- answer current.

When an update occurs at cell p to new value val:
1. If p is not dirty:
   - H_p = E0[p] + sum_{v∈S, v<p} s_v * T(v,p). But if we maintain Hcur for dirty cells only, p not dirty so we need compute H_p. We can compute it by querying over dirty cells: H_p = E0[p] + ∑_{v∈S, v<p} s_v * T(v,p). This is O(B) if T query O(1). Then add p to S with delta = val - A0[p], s_p = delta * H_p.
   - Update Hcur for all dirty u>p: Hcur[u] += s_p * T(p,u).
   - Update answer: if p != end, ans += A_current[end] * s_p * T(p,end)? Wait answer = A_end * H_end. H_end is Hcur[end] if end dirty? Actually H_end = E0[end] + ∑_{v∈S} s_v*T(v,end). This formula holds regardless of end dirty. A_current[end] = A0[end] if end not dirty else current value. If p is not end, A_end unchanged, so ans += A_end * s_p * T(p,end). If p is end, then A_end changes from old to val; answer = A_end * H_end. H_end excludes end, so unaffected by p's own delta. Need update ans = val * H_end (where H_end includes sources before end, not p). If p=end and new dirty, H_end = H_p computed before adding p? Since H_end excludes end, and p=end, sources before end do not include p. So H_end remains same. Then ans = val * H_end. Also we should not add s_p*T(p,end) because p is not < end. So handle end specially.
2. If p is already dirty:
   - old_delta = delta[p], old_s = old_delta * Hcur[p].
   - new_delta = val - A0[p], new_s = new_delta * Hcur[p].
   - If p != end:
       diff_s = new_s - old_s.
       For all dirty u>p: Hcur[u] += diff_s * T(p,u).
       ans += A_end * diff_s * T(p,end).
   - If p == end:
       H_end unchanged; ans = val * H_end. (delta[end] not used for answer except A_end factor; but for future sources, end has no downstream, so no need update s? If end dirty and later no cells after, s_end never used. We can still store delta but not needed.)
   - Update delta[p] = new_delta, s[p] = new_s (if needed).

This incremental algorithm per update is O(B) plus O(1) T queries, where B is number of dirty cells in current block. But we also need recompute base at block start O(N). Number of blocks Q/B. Total O(N*Q/B + Q*B). Optimize B ~ sqrt(N) ~447 gives O(N*Q/447 + Q*447) ≈ 9e7 + 9e7 = 1.8e8 operations. In Python maybe borderline but possible if optimized and T queries O(1). Need also compute T(p,u) for arbitrary pairs p,u quickly.

Let F0[x] = sum start->x product including x.
Let C0[x] = sum x->end product excluding x.

X_c[s] = G(u,(s,c)). This can be computed easily: for s=r, 1; for s>r, product of A0 along column c from r+1 to s; for s<r, 0. So X_c is a vector. Thus if we have a segment tree of column transformation matrices, we can compute G(u,n) by applying product to a vector: O(K^2 log L) per query. Still too slow for 8e7 queries.

A matrix is called (upper) semiseparable if entries above diagonal can be represented as u_i v_j. Product of semiseparable matrices may be semiseparable with higher rank. Our T_c is semiseparable of rank 1? Entries T_c[i][j] = product_{k=j}^i a_k for i>=j. This can be written as L_i * R_j where L_i = product_{k=1}^i a_k, R_j = 1/product_{k=1}^{j-1} a_k if invertible. So rank 1 semiseparable. Product of rank-1 semiseparable matrices may have rank increasing by number of factors. After L factors, rank can be L, up to K. So applying product may require O(K^2) in worst case. But maybe the rank is at most K, and K is small. Still O(K^2).

Let's consider another representation: The DP recurrence can be seen as a linear recurrence in the row index for each column. The product over columns corresponds to a 2D linear recurrence. Maybe the final answer can be updated in O(K) using the "transfer matrix" but with the observation that we only need the last row, not the whole vector? Let's see.

Answer = F[H][W]. If we process columns, F at last row of each column depends on the entire state vector. But maybe we can maintain a scalar recurrence for the last row using the previous last row and some auxiliary quantities. For H=2, state size 2, answer depends on both. For general K, answer depends on all K state entries. But perhaps we can maintain a "linear functional" that evolves with O(K) per column. The final answer is the last component of the state vector. When a column transformation changes, the change in the final answer can be computed by propagating a "dual vector" backwards. This is promising!

For a linear system X_L = M X_0, answer = e_K^T M e_1. A point update in column c changes M_c to M_c'. The change in answer can be expressed using the forward state at column c-1 and the backward dual vector at column c. Specifically, if M = M_right * M_c * M_left, then Δanswer = e_K^T M_right ΔM_c M_left e_1. Let v = M_left e_1 (forward state before column c), and u = (M_right)^T e_K (backward vector). Then Δanswer = u^T ΔM_c v. Since M_c is a column transformation, ΔM_c has special structure; computing u^T ΔM_c v might be O(K) or O(K^2). If we maintain forward state vectors at all column boundaries and backward vectors, we can answer updates in O(K) maybe. But after an update, forward states to the right and backward states to the left change. We would need update them, potentially O(L*K). However, maybe we can use a segment tree to maintain these states? This is similar to dynamic product.

But there is a known technique: For a product of matrices, to support point updates and query a bilinear form, you can use a segment tree where each node stores the product matrix; no escape. Unless matrices have low rank.

Let's examine ΔM_c. M_c is upper triangular with entries product along column. If A[r,c] changes, how does M_c change? M_c = (I - A S)^{-1} A. A is diagonal. The change in diagonal A at one row might produce a rank-1 update to M_c? Let's compute. Let old column values a_i, new a_i' at row r. The transformation T_c maps input vector X to output Y via recurrence Y_i = a_i (Y_{i-1}+X_i). This is a linear system. Changing a_r affects Y_i for i>=r. The matrix ΔM_c might be rank-1? Let's test K=2, change a1. Old M = [[a1,0],[a1*a2,a2]]. New M' = [[a1',0],[a1'*a2,a2]]. Δ = [[d,0],[d*a2,0]] where d=a1'-a1. This is rank 1 (column 1 times [1,a2]^T? Actually rows: row1 [d,0], row2 [d*a2,0] = [1,a2]^T * d * [1,0]). Change a2: old M [[a1,0],[a1*a2,a2]], new [[a1,0],[a1*a2',a2']]. Δ = [[0,0],[a1*d2,d2]] = [0,1]^T * d2 * [a1,1]? Rank 1. In general, changing one diagonal entry in the recurrence likely results in a rank-1 update to the transfer matrix! This is huge.

Let's verify. The recurrence Y_i = a_i(Y_{i-1}+X_i). This is a linear system (I - A S) Y = A X, where S is lower shift. M = (I - A S)^{-1} A. If A changes at row r by d, then A' = A + d e_r e_r^T. ΔM = (I - A'S)^{-1} A' - (I - A S)^{-1} A. There is a Sherman-Morrison formula; the change may be rank 1? Let's derive for K=3 maybe. Changing a_r affects entries M[i][j] for i>=r and j<=r? Because product along column includes a_r only if j<=r<=i. So ΔM[i][j] = (product of other factors) * d for j<=r<=i, else 0. This is an outer product of a vector U (for rows i>=r) and V (for columns j<=r). Specifically, ΔM = d * U_row * V_col^T, where U_i = product_{k=r+1..i} a_k (for i>=r, U_r=1), V_j = product_{k=j..r-1} a_k (for j<=r, V_r=1). Yes! This is exactly rank 1 (or rank 1 in the submatrix). Great! This is the key.

Thus a point update to a cell in a column changes the column transfer matrix by a rank-1 matrix. We can use this to update the product and answer efficiently with segment tree storing some vectors? Let's explore.

We have product M = M_right * M_c * M_left. ΔM_c = u v^T (Kx1 times 1xK) where u depends on rows below/equal r in column c, v depends on columns above/equal r. Then ΔM = M_right * (u v^T) * M_left = (M_right u) * (v^T M_left). This is a rank-1 update to the full product matrix M! Wait M is the product of all column transformations from left boundary to right boundary. If we maintain the full product matrix M (KxK), a point update changes M by a rank-1 matrix. a = M_right * u (size K)
b = (M_left)^T * v (size K)
Then M_new = M_old + a * b^T.
This is O(K^2) per update, not O(K^3)! And we only need the full product matrix M (KxK). Memory O(K^2) ~100k, fine. Could we maintain M_left and M_right as full matrices for the current column and update them when moving by one using rank-1? When moving right:
- M_left_new = M_left * M_c. This is a matrix multiplication O(K^3) if done naively. But M_c is special; multiplying M_left by M_c (right multiply) can be done O(K^2) using recurrence? Let's check. M_left * T_c: For each row of M_left, apply T_c? Actually (M_left * T_c)[i][j] = ∑_k M_left[i][k] T_c[k][j]. T_c upper triangular. We can compute for each row i in O(K^2) total? Maybe O(K^2) per row? Let's derive. Right multiplication by T_c: For a matrix A (KxK), B = A * T_c. T_c[k][j] = product_{r=j..k} a_r for j<=k. Then B[i][j] = ∑_{k=j..K} A[i][k] * prod_{r=j..k} a_r. This can be computed for each i,j with recurrence over k? For fixed i, define S_j = B[i][j]. Recurrence from top? Let's derive:
B[i][j] = a_j * (A[i][j] + B[i][j+1]?) Because product from j to k = a_j * product from j+1 to k. Sum over k>=j A[i][k] * a_j * prod_{j+1..k} = a_j * ( A[i][j] + ∑_{k>j} A[i][k] prod_{j+1..k} ) = a_j * (A[i][j] + B[i][j+1]). Yes! So right multiplication by T_c can be computed for each row i in O(K) by iterating j from K down to 1: B[i][j] = a_j * (A[i][j] + B[i][j+1]). Total O(K^2). Great! Similarly left multiplication by T_c is O(K^2) as before. So multiplying a matrix by T_c is O(K^2), not O(K^3).

Thus maintaining M_left = M_left * M_c when moving right is O(K^2). Moving left: need remove last column from M_left, i.e., M_left_old = M_left_new * T_c^{-1}? T_c may not be invertible if zeros. But we can instead maintain suffix products similarly. Maybe we can maintain both prefix and suffix matrices for the current column using a "two-stack" SWAG where push/pop are O(K^2)? Let's think.

The current column w moves by ±1. We can maintain:
- Prefix product P = product of columns 1..w-1.
- Suffix product S = product of columns w+1..L.
When moving right:
  P_new = P * T_w (include old current)
  current transformation becomes T_{w+1}
  S_new = S without T_{w+1}. Need pop from front of suffix.
When moving left:
  P_new = P without T_{w-1} (pop from back of prefix)
  current becomes T_{w-1}
  S_new = T_w * S (include old current? Actually if moving left from w to w-1, new suffix columns w..L = T_w * old S? Old S was columns w+1..L. New S should columns w..L = T_w * S. So push front to suffix.)
So we need a data structure for a sequence of column transformations that supports push/pop at both ends? The prefix is a stack (push back, pop back) and suffix is a queue (push front, pop front). We can maintain aggregate product for each with a SWAG (sliding window aggregation) that supports push and pop in amortized O(K^2) per operation. Since each move is one step, we can maintain P and S with two stacks each? Let's design.

For prefix P (columns 1..w-1), we only append to the right when moving right, and pop from the right when moving left. That's a stack. We can maintain the product of the stack as we push/pop. To support pop, we need store the stack elements and their aggregate products. Since we only pop the most recently pushed, we can simply maintain a list of column transformations and the cumulative product from bottom to top? Actually if we push T, new aggregate = old_aggregate * T (right multiply). If we pop, we need the previous aggregate. We can store for each stack element the aggregate after pushing it. Then pop restores previous aggregate. This is O(K^2) per push/pop for matrix multiplication. Memory O(L*K^2) if storing aggregate for each element? But we only need aggregates for current stack; when moving right, we push and store aggregate; when moving left, pop and discard. The stack depth can be up to L. Storing aggregate matrix for each depth would be O(L*K^2) memory, too much. But we don't need all aggregates if we only pop the top; we can store the list of column transformations and recompute aggregate from scratch on pop? That would be O(depth*K^2). Not good.

However, we can store for each stack element the aggregate matrix at that point. Memory O(L*K^2) worst. But L*K^2 = N*K. For K=447,L=447, 447*200k=89M ints, too high. But maybe we can store only the column transformations (O(L*K)=N) and use a different method to recompute aggregate on pop? No.

For suffix S (columns w+1..L), moving right pops front, moving left pushes front. A queue can be implemented with two stacks (SWAG) to support push/pop and aggregate in amortized O(K^2) per operation, storing aggregates for stack elements. Again memory O(L*K^2) in worst case? SWAG stores aggregates for each element in the stacks; total O(number of elements * K^2). Too high.

But maybe we don't need to maintain P and S as full matrices for all positions; we only need them to update the full product M when the current column's transformation changes. If we can maintain P and S with O(K^2) per move and O(K^2) memory per current? Let's see.

Maybe we can maintain P and S as full matrices for the current column only, and when moving, update them using the column transformations. To move right:

Let's revisit the rank-1 update idea and see if we can maintain the full product M and also answer updates in O(K^2) without needing M_left/M_right by using the moving position and storing prefix/suffix products in a balanced way with O(K^2) memory per level? Maybe we can use a segment tree where each node stores its product matrix, but we only need to query M_left and M_right for the current column. Let's think.

We have a static segment tree over columns (but node matrices change when columns update). We can maintain the prefix product P = product [1, w-1] and suffix S = product [w+1, L]. When moving cursor from w to w+1:
- P_new = P * T_w. T_w is the current transformation of column w (which we can store). This is one matrix multiply by T_w, O(K^2). Maybe we can reduce K^2 by using the fact that we only need the full product M's first column? Wait for answer, M[K][1]. When updating M_c by rank-1, M_new = M_old + a b^T. To update M, we need a and b. a = M_right * u, b = (M_left)^T v. These are vectors. We don't need full M_left/M_right, only their application to specific vectors u and v. u and v depend on the updated cell within the column. M_right * u: u is a vector that is nonzero only in rows >= r (for down/right? Actually u_i = product of A in column c from r+1 to i for i>=r). This is like a source in column c. M_right maps a vector at column c to final vector. If we had the full product M_right, applying to u is O(K^2). But maybe we can compute M_right * u by propagating u through the suffix columns. That's O(L*K) if done naively. Not good.

But note u has a special form: it is the column transformation's response to a change at row r. It is a vector of suffix products down the column. Applying M_right to u is equivalent to computing the effect of a source at (r,c) on the final state. This is exactly the Green's function from the updated cell to the end. Similarly, b is the Green's function from start to the updated cell. These are the E and C values we discussed! If we maintained E_current[p] and C_current[p] for the current position, we could update answer easily. But after update, E/C for future positions change.

Maybe the moving position allows us to maintain E and C at the current position incrementally? When Takahashi moves to an adjacent cell, the new position's E and C can be computed from neighboring cells' E and C? Let's see.

E_current[p] = sum paths start->p excluding p. For a cell p, E[p] = F[up] + F[left] where F are current DP including cells. If we know F at neighbors, we can compute E[p] in O(1). F[p] = A[p]*E[p]. So if we maintain F for all cells, moving to adjacent cell and updating its value, we can recompute F for that cell and maybe propagate. But F for downstream cells changes. However, maybe we only need E and C at the current position for the next update. The current position moves one step; the new position is adjacent. Its E depends on F of its up/left neighbors. Those neighbors may be upstream of the updated cell? If the move is down/right, the new cell is downstream of the previous cell; its E may have changed due to the update. If the move is up/left, the new cell is upstream; its E may not have changed? Let's analyze.

Takahashi's path is a walk. Suppose he updates cell p, then moves to q adjacent. The next update will be at q. To compute the answer change for updating q, we need E_current[q] and C_current[q] (or H and suffix). If q is upstream of p (moving up/left), then E[q] is not affected by the update at p (since p is downstream of q). If q is downstream of p (moving down/right), then E[q] is affected by p. But maybe we can compute E[q] from E[p] and local values? For q = p + down, E[q] = F[p] + F[left of q]? Actually E[q] excluding q = F[p] + F[p_h, q_w-1]. F[p] is known (A[p]*E[p]). F[left of q] may be affected by p if left of q is downstream? If q is down of p, left of q is (p_h, p_w+1?) Wait q=(h+1,w). Left neighbor is (h+1,w-1), which is not necessarily downstream of p? It is down-left of p, not reachable from p (would require left then down, but from p down to q then left? Actually (h+1,w-1) is not reachable from p by down/right moves? From p=(h,w), down to (h+1,w), left to (h+1,w-1) is not allowed (left move). So (h+1,w-1) is not downstream of p; its F is unaffected. Thus E[q] = F[p] (updated) + F[h+1,w-1] (unchanged). So we can compute E[q] in O(1)! Similarly, for moves:
- If move down: q=(h+1,w). E[q] = F[h,w] + F[h+1,w-1] (left neighbor). F[h,w] is the updated cell (current p). F[h+1,w-1] is not downstream of p? It is not reachable from p, so unchanged. Good.
- If move right: q=(h,w+1). E[q] = F[h-1,w+1] (up neighbor) + F[h,w]. F[h,w] updated; up neighbor not downstream of p? (h-1,w+1) is not reachable from p, unchanged.
- If move up: q=(h-1,w). E[q] = F[h-2,w] + F[h-1,w-1]. Neither is downstream of p? p=(h,w), q is up. F[h-1,w-1] is up-left, not reachable from p. F[h-2,w] above q, not downstream. Unchanged.
- If move left: q=(h,w-1). E[q] = F[h-1,w-1] + F[h,w-2]. Unchanged.
So E at the new position can be computed in O(1) from the current F values, without propagating! This is huge.

What about C_current[q] (suffix excluding q)? C[q] = sum paths q->end excluding q. If q is upstream of p (moving up/left), then p is downstream of q, so C[q] is affected by the update at p. If q is downstream of p (moving down/right), C[q] is not affected by p? Let's check. C[q] depends on cells downstream of q. If q is down/right of p, then p is upstream of q, not downstream, so C[q] unaffected. If q is up/left of p, p is downstream of q, so C[q] affected. Can we compute C[q] in O(1) from neighboring C values?
C[q] = sum paths q->end excluding q = (if down exists) A[down]*C[down]? Wait earlier C[x] excluding x = A[down]*C[down] + A[right]*C[right]? Let's define C[x] = sum paths x->end product excluding x. Recurrence C[x] = (if down) A[down]*C[down]? Actually C[down] excludes down, so path q->down->... product excluding q = A[down] * (product excluding down) = A[down]*C[down]. Similarly right. So C[q] = A[down]*C[down] + A[right]*C[right].
If q is up of p (p=(h,w), q=(h-1,w)), then down neighbor of q is p (updated). So C[q] depends on A[p]*C[p]. C[p] is suffix excluding p, unaffected by update at p? C[p] excludes p, depends on cells downstream of p, so unchanged. A[p] updated. Thus C[q] can be computed if we know C[p] and the other neighbor (right of q) which is not downstream of p? Right of q=(h-1,w+1), not downstream of p? p=(h,w), (h-1,w+1) is up-right, not reachable from p, unchanged. So C[q] = A[p]*C[p] + A[h-1,w+1]*C[h-1,w+1]. We know A[p] updated and C[p] unchanged. Good.
If q is left of p (p=(h,w), q=(h,w-1)), right neighbor of q is p. C[q] = A[p]*C[p] + A[h+1,w-1]*C[h+1,w-1] (down neighbor), unchanged. So C[q] computable O(1).

This is the key! Because the update position moves one cell, we can maintain the DP values F and suffix C for all cells? Wait to compute E[q] we used F of some neighbors; to compute C[q] we used C of some neighbors. If we maintain F and C for all cells, we can compute E and C at the new position in O(1). But after updating A[p], F and C for many cells change. However, maybe we don't need to update all F and C; we only need F and C values for cells that will be queried as neighbors in future moves. The walk visits cells; when we move, we need F and C of certain neighboring cells. Are those affected by previous updates? We argued the specific neighbors used are not downstream/upstream of the updated cell, so they remain unchanged. Let's verify more generally.

We maintain F_current[h][w] = sum paths start->(h,w) product including cell, and C_current[h][w] = sum paths (h,w)->end product excluding cell. When we update A[p], F changes for all cells downstream of p (including p). C changes for all cells upstream of p (including p? C[p] excludes p, so C[p] unchanged; but C of upstream cells changes). For the next position q adjacent to p:
- To compute E[q] = F[up(q)] + F[left(q)] (with F outside 0). These two neighbors of q are either p (if q is down/right of p) or not reachable from p (if q is up/left). If one is p, F[p] is updated and we know it. The other is not downstream of p, so unchanged. Thus we can compute E[q] using stored F values (which are correct for unchanged cells and updated p).
- To compute C[q] = A[down(q)]*C[down(q)] + A[right(q)]*C[right(q)]. If q is up/left of p, one neighbor is p, C[p] unchanged, A[p] updated. The other is not upstream of p? Need ensure its C is unchanged. If q is up of p, right(q)=(h-1,w+1). Is (h-1,w+1) upstream of p? Upstream means can reach p via down/right. From (h-1,w+1) to p=(h,w): need down and left, left not allowed. So not upstream. Thus C unchanged. If q is left of p, down(q)=(h+1,w-1) to p=(h,w): right and up? Up not allowed. Not upstream. Good. If q is down/right of p, then C[q]'s neighbors are downstream of q, not including p, so unaffected by p (p upstream). So C[q] unchanged. Thus we can compute C[q] from stored values.

This suggests we can process updates online by maintaining only F and C for all cells? But F and C for many cells become stale after updates. However, the only F/C values we ever read are:
- F[p] for the updated cell (we compute it).
- F of certain neighbors of the next position that are not downstream of p, so they should be unchanged from initial? Wait over many updates, a cell may be read multiple times. Is it possible that a cell's F changes due to an update at some other cell, and later we read it as an "unchanged" neighbor? We argued for the immediate next move, the neighbor is not downstream of the current updated p. But over the entire walk, a cell's F could have changed due to an earlier update that is upstream of it. If we never updated that cell's stored F, it would be stale. We need to ensure that when we read F of a cell, it is either a cell we have updated (and thus recomputed) or a cell whose F has never changed since we last stored it. But a cell's F can change due to updates at cells upstream of it, which may not be adjacent to it. If we don't propagate, stored F becomes stale. Example: update at (1,1), then move right to (1,2) and update. F at (2,2) changes due to update at (1,1), but we never recomputed F[2,2]. Later, if we need F[2,2] as a neighbor, it's stale. So maintaining only local F/C is not enough.

But maybe the walk's movement ensures that when we need F of a cell, it has been updated recently or is on the boundary? Not necessarily. Consider H=2,W=3, start (2,2). Update (1,2) (up), then move right to (1,3), update. Later move down? F[2,3] changes due to updates at (1,2),(1,3), but if we never visited (2,3), stale. Need update F for downstream cells.

However, maybe we can maintain F and C lazily using the fact that the current position is a walk and we only need F/C at the current position and its neighbors. Let's test the local update idea more. When we update A[p], we can compute F[p] = A[p]*E[p]. We know E[p] from previous position. But F of downstream cells changes. If later we move into a downstream cell, its E depends on F of its up/left neighbors, which may include the updated cell or other downstream cells whose F we haven't updated. Let's develop this.

We process updates in blocks of size B. At the start of a block, we have the current grid values (base for the block). We need to answer after each update in the block. Let's define more precisely.

X_c = T_{c-1} ... T_1 e1? Wait state at column c-1 boundary (before column c) or at column c? Let's set boundary between columns. Let X_c be the state vector at column c-1 (input to column c). Then change in answer due to ΔT_c is Y_c^T ΔT_c X_c. Good.

So we need X_c (input to current column) and Y_c (backward from after current column to end).

When we update a cell in column c, T_c changes by rank-1. X_c and Y_c are unaffected by T_c (X_c depends on columns <c, Y_c on columns >c). So we can compute answer change using X_c,Y_c. After updating T_c, we need to move to next column c' = c + delta (delta ∈ {-1,0,1}). We need X_{c'} and Y_{c'}.

How to update X and Y when moving the column pointer?

- If move right to c+1:
   X_{c+1} = T_c X_c (the state at column c, which is the input to column c+1). We can compute this in O(K) by applying the column transformation T_c to vector X_c! Great.

  A_{c+1} = T_c A_c (O(K)).
  B_{c+1} = (T_L ... T_{c+2})^T e_K. We have B_c = (T_L ... T_{c+1})^T e_K = T_{c+1}^T B_{c+1}. Need inverse. So B moves left easily? If moving left:
  B_{c-1} = (T_L ... T_c)^T e_K = T_c^T B_c? Wait B_{c-1} = (T_L ... T_c)^T e_K = T_c^T (T_L ... T_{c+1})^T e_K = T_c^T B_c. So moving left, B can be updated by applying T_c^T to B_c (O(K) if T_c^T application O(K)? T_c^T is lower triangular with entries product; applying to vector may be O(K^2) or O(K)? Let's check. T_c is upper triangular; T_c^T is lower triangular. Applying T_c^T to a vector y: z_j = ∑_{i>=j} T_c[i][j] y_i = ∑_{i>=j} (prod_{k=j..i} a_k) y_i. This can be computed by recurrence from bottom: z_j = a_j * (y_j + z_{j+1})? Let's derive: z_j = a_j * y_j + a_j*a_{j+1} y_{j+1} + ... = a_j * (y_j + ∑_{i>j} prod_{k=j+1..i} a_k y_i) = a_j * (y_j + z_{j+1}). Yes! So applying T_c^T to a vector is O(K). Great.

Thus:
- Moving right: A_{c+1} = T_c A_c (O(K)). B_{c+1} cannot be updated without inverse.
- Moving left: B_{c-1} = T_c^T B_c (O(K)). A_{c-1} needs inverse.

So we can maintain A when moving right, and B when moving left. But we need both at the current column. If the walk moves both directions, we need a way to get the other vector. We can maintain a stack of A vectors when moving right, and a stack of B vectors when moving left? Let's think.

We can maintain A_c for all columns visited? When moving right, we can compute A_{c+1} and push it. When moving left, we can pop A to get A_{c-1} (since A_{c-1} was stored). Similarly, when moving left, we can compute B_{c-1} and push; when moving right, pop B. This is a classic two-stack approach! Define for each boundary between columns c and c+1? Let's use:

What if we compute T(v,u) for all pairs in S using the "divide and conquer on the bounding box" in O(area log area + B^2)? There is a known CDQ algorithm for offline point-to-point path queries in a DAG:
To answer queries (v,u) asking sum of paths from v to u, we can do divide and conquer on rows/columns. For a rectangle, split by middle row/column. For queries whose endpoints lie on different sides, the path must cross the separator; we can compute DP from separator to all cells and answer in O(1). Recurse. Complexity O((N+Q) log N). This is for static grid and many queries. We have per block B^2 queries (pairs among dirty cells) and base grid fixed per block. We could answer all T queries in a block using CDQ in O((area + B^2) log N) instead of B*area. This might be feasible! Let's explore.

Static grid, base values. We have a set of query pairs (v,u) with v<u, asking T(v,u) (sum of paths from v to u excluding endpoints). We can answer all such queries offline using divide-and-conquer on the grid.

Standard CDQ on a grid for path sums:
- Consider a rectangle of cells. Split it by a vertical line (or horizontal) into left and right parts.
- For queries where v and u are both in left or both in right, recurse.
- For queries where v is in left and u in right (or vice versa? Since v<u, if columns differ, v left of u; if same column, vertical), any path from v to u must cross the split line. We can compute contributions by enumerating crossing points on the split line.
If split by a column mid, paths from v (left) to u (right) cross the mid column at some row. We can compute:
  - For each cell x on the split column, sum from v to x (including x?) and from x to u (excluding x?) and combine.
But doing this for all queries naively O(num_queries * K) per level. Total O((N + Q*K) log L). With Q_pairs = B^2 ~ 5e5 per block, K~447, too high. But maybe we can split by rows/columns to make separator size small? The separator size is min(height,width). In worst square, K~447. Q_pairs*K per level ~5e5*447=223M per block, impossible.

Similarly, right stack contains columns c+1..L. To move left, we add column c to the right stack? Let's define.

We maintain:
- Left stack: columns 1..c-1, with aggregate vector A_c = product of these columns applied to e1. When moving right, push column c onto left stack; new aggregate A_{c+1} = T_c A_c (O(K)). When moving left, pop column c-1 from left stack; restore previous aggregate (stored).
- Right stack: columns c+1..L, with aggregate vector B_c = product of these columns applied to e_K? More precisely B_c = (T_L ... T_{c+1})^T e_K. This is a vector obtained by applying the suffix transformations (in reverse order) to e_K. If we store the right stack as columns c+1..L in order, the aggregate B_c can be maintained. When moving left, we add column c to the right stack (since new suffix for c-1 includes column c). How to update B? New B_{c-1} = (T_L ... T_c)^T e_K = T_c^T B_c. So B_new = T_c^T B_c (O(K)). When moving right, we pop column c+1 from right stack; restore previous B (stored).

This is perfect! We don't need inverse. We maintain A for the left stack and B for the right stack. The current column c is the boundary between the stacks. The answer is B_c^T (T_c A_c)? Wait A_c is input to column c (columns <c). B_c is suffix after column c (columns >c). For an update in column c, we need A_c and B_c. We have them. To compute answer before update, it's B_c^T (T_c A_c). We can maintain answer separately.

When moving:
- Right: c -> c+1. Push column c onto left stack: A_{c+1} = T_c A_c. Pop column c+1 from right stack: B_{c+1} is the stored aggregate after removing T_{c+1}. We need the right stack to support popping from the front? Let's define right stack carefully.

Right stack should contain columns c+1, c+2, ..., L in that order (left to right). The aggregate B_c = (T_L ... T_{c+1})^T e_K. This is like applying the columns from right to left: start with e_K, for col = L down to c+1: B = T_col^T B. So if we store the right stack as a stack with top at c+1? When moving right, we need to remove the leftmost column c+1 from the aggregate. This is a pop from the "front" of the sequence. If we push columns onto the right stack when moving left, we add column c to the front. So right stack needs push_front and pop_front. We can implement it as a stack if we store columns in reverse order? Let's define right stack as a list of columns from L down to c+1? The aggregate B_c is computed by processing columns from L down to c+1. If we store the stack with top being column c+1? Let's see.

Suppose right stack contains columns in order [c+1, c+2, ..., L] with top at c+1? To compute B_c, we need to process from L down to c+1, i.e., reverse order. If we store the stack with top at L, then pushing/popping at the right end corresponds to moving left/right? Let's define:
- Right stack top = column L? When moving left (c -> c-1), we need to add column c to the suffix. The new suffix is columns c..L. The aggregate B_{c-1} = T_c^T B_c. This is like pushing column c onto the "front" of the suffix, but if our stack top is L, we would push at the bottom. Not stack-friendly.
Instead, store right stack as a stack of columns in decreasing order: top = c+1? Let's test.

We want operations:
- Move left: add column c to the front of right stack. New aggregate = T_c^T old_aggregate. This is a push operation where the new aggregate is computed from old by applying T_c^T. If we store the aggregate for each stack depth, we can push.
- Move right: remove column c+1 from the front of right stack. This is a pop operation; we need the aggregate before that column was pushed. If we store aggregates for each depth, pop restores previous.

Thus right stack can be a standard stack where the top corresponds to the front of the suffix (column c+1). When moving left, we push column c (which becomes the new front) and compute new aggregate = T_c^T old_aggregate. When moving right, we pop the front (column c+1) and restore the aggregate before it was pushed. This works! We just need to store for each push the aggregate before/after. The stack elements are columns in order of front to back? Actually if we push column c when moving left, the stack top is c, below it is c+1, etc. So the stack stores columns [c, c+1, ..., L] with top = c. The aggregate at depth d is B for the suffix starting at top. When moving right, we pop top (c+1? Wait if current c, right stack top is c+1. After moving right to c+1, we need suffix starting at c+2, so pop top c+1. The new aggregate is the one stored below. Good.)

Similarly, left stack stores columns [1, ..., c-1] with top = c-1. Aggregate A_c is product of all columns in left stack applied to e1. When moving right, push column c (new top), new aggregate = T_c old_aggregate. When moving left, pop top c-1, restore previous aggregate.

This is excellent! We can maintain A and B with stacks of vectors, O(K) per move, memory O((left_depth + right_depth)*K). The total stack depth over time? At any time, left stack size = c-1, right stack size = L-c. The sum is L-1 <= L <= 2e5. But we store a vector for each column in the stacks. That's O(L*K) = O(N) memory! Wait, left stack stores aggregates for each prefix length up to c-1. Right stack stores aggregates for each suffix length. The total number of stored vectors across both stacks is (c-1) + (L-c) = L-1. Each vector length K. So memory O(L*K) = O(N) <= 2e5 ints! This is brilliant. We don't store Q*K, only L*K because the stacks partition all columns. When moving, we push/pop and store aggregates for each column. The stacks contain all columns? Let's verify.

Initially, we need to build the stacks for the starting column sh. We can initialize left stack with columns 1..sh-1 and right stack with columns sh+1..L, storing aggregates. This takes O(L*K) time and memory. Then each move updates one stack by push/pop, O(K). The total number of stored vectors is L-1, not Q*K. Perfect!

Now, how to handle updates and the answer?

We maintain:
- `left_stack`: list of (column_index, aggregate_vector) for columns 1..c-1? Actually we need the aggregate vector after including up to that column. Let's define:
  For left stack, if it contains columns [1..c-1] with top = c-1, the aggregate vector `A` at top is T_{c-1} ... T_1 e1 = input to column c. We can store for each depth the aggregate vector. When we push a new column col, new_agg = apply_column(col, old_agg) (T_col * old_agg). Store (col, new_agg). When we pop, A = previous stored agg.
- `right_stack`: contains columns [c+1..L] with top = c+1. The aggregate vector `B` at top is (T_L ... T_{c+1})^T e_K. We can store for each depth the aggregate vector. When we push a new column col (moving left, col=c), new_agg = apply_transpose_column(col, old_agg) (T_col^T * old_agg). Store (col, new_agg). When we pop, B = previous stored agg.

Initially, we need to build these stacks for the starting column c = sw (after transposition). We can build left stack by iterating columns 1..c-1 and pushing; right stack by iterating columns L down to c+1? Let's define right stack top should be c+1. To build, we can start with B = e_K (empty suffix after L). Then for col from L down to c+1: push col onto right stack with new_agg = T_col^T * B. After loop, top is c+1 and B is suffix after c. This stores columns in order [c+1, c+2, ..., L] with top c+1. Good. Complexity O((L-c)*K). Left stack: start A=e1, for col=1..c-1: push col with new_agg = T_col * A. Complexity O((c-1)*K). Total O(L*K)=O(N). Good.

Now, we also need to maintain the current column transformation T_c and the current row position. The update occurs at cell (h,w) in the current column c=w (after possible transpose). We need to update the value A[h][c] in the column transformation T_c. We have A_c (input to column c) and B_c (suffix after column c). The current answer before update is `ans = B_c^T (T_c A_c)`? We can maintain `ans` as the current total answer. Initially compute ans by applying all columns: after building stacks, we can compute `mid = apply_column(c, A)` (T_c A_c), then ans = dot(B, mid). Or we can compute ans from full DP at start. We'll maintain ans.

When updating cell (h,c) from old_val to new_val:
- We need compute the change in the column transformation T_c due to changing A[h][c] from old to new. We derived ΔT_c = u v^T (rank-1). Then Δans = B_c^T ΔT_c A_c = (B_c^T u) * (v^T A_c). This is O(K) if we can compute u and v.
- We also need update the stored column transformation T_c (the values A[:,c]) for future applications/moves. We'll update the grid value.

Let's derive u and v precisely.

Column transformation T_c maps input vector X (length K, rows 1..K) to output Y via:
Y[0]=0
for i=1..K: Y[i] = a_i * (Y[i-1] + X[i])
where a_i = A[i][c].

If a_r changes by delta = new - old, how does T_c change? We want ΔT_c such that ΔY = ΔT_c X.

For i < r, Y[i] unchanged because recurrence doesn't involve a_r.
For i = r, Y[r] = a_r (Y[r-1] + X[r]). ΔY[r] = delta * (Y[r-1] + X[r]).
For i > r, Y[i] = a_i (Y[i-1] + X[i]). ΔY[i] = a_i ΔY[i-1] (since a_i unchanged). Thus ΔY[i] = delta * (Y[r-1] + X[r]) * ∏_{k=r+1}^i a_k.

So ΔY = u * s, where s = Y[r-1] + X[r] = (T_c X)[r-1] + X[r], and u_i = 0 for i<r, u_r=1, u_i = ∏_{k=r+1}^i a_k for i>r.

But in the rank-1 form ΔT_c = u v^T, we need v such that v^T X = s = X[r] + Y[r-1]. Y[r-1] depends on X[1..r-1] via the column transformation. We can express Y[r-1] = ∑_{j=1}^{r-1} (∏_{k=j}^{r-1} a_k) X[j]. Therefore v_j = (∏_{k=j}^{r-1} a_k) for j<=r-1, and v_r = 1, v_j=0 for j>r. Check v^T X = ∑_{j<r} prod_{j..r-1} a_j X[j] + X[r] = Y[r-1] + X[r]. Yes.

So:
- v is a vector of length K: for j=1..r-1, v[j-1] = product_{k=j}^{r-1} a_k; v[r-1] = 1; for j>r, 0.
- u is a vector: u[r-1] = 1; for i>r, u[i-1] = product_{k=r+1}^{i} a_k; for i<r, 0.

Then Δans = delta * (B_c^T u) * (v^T A_c). Because ΔT_c = u v^T.

We can compute `dot_Bu = sum(B[i]*u[i])` and `dot_vA = sum(v[j]*A[j])` in O(K). But note v^T A_c = Y[r-1] + A_c[r]? Wait A_c is input vector X. Yes.

Alternatively, we can compute `s = Y[r-1] + X[r]` where Y = T_c A_c. We can maintain Y? We can compute Y = apply_column(c, A) O(K) when needed. But for update, we need Y[r-1]. We can compute Y on the fly O(K) per update. That's fine. Then dot_Bu also O(K). Total O(K) per update.

After updating ans, we need update the column values a_r = new. This changes T_c. The stacks' aggregate vectors for left/right do not include T_c (left stack includes columns <c, right stack includes columns >c), so A and B remain correct. The stored column transformation T_c is updated for future apply_column calls and for when this column is pushed into left/right stacks upon moving.

Now, after the update, Takahashi moves to an adjacent cell. We need update the current column c and row h, and adjust the stacks.

The move direction d_i is given. We have current position (h,w) before move? Actually the problem: "Move one cell in direction d_i. Then, let destination be (h,w), and set A_{h,w}=a_i." So the update happens after moving. Our current position should be the destination. We can process as: first move to new cell, then update. Or update at current? Let's align.

Input: starts at (sh,sw). For each change: move one cell in direction d_i, destination becomes (h,w), set A_{h,w}=a_i. So before the i-th change, Takahashi is at some cell. He moves to adjacent cell, then updates that cell. So the updated cell is the new position. We should move first, then update. Our state should represent the current position before the move? Let's define at the start of each query, current position is the cell that will be the destination? Initially at (sh,sw). For first query, he moves from (sh,sw) to dest, then updates dest. So the updated cell is dest, not initial. After the update, his position is dest. For next query, he moves from dest to new dest. So the sequence of updated cells is the walk positions after each move. The current position before a move is the previous updated cell (or start for first). We can process each query as:
- Move from current position to new position (h,w) by applying d_i.
- Update cell (h,w).
- Set current position = (h,w).

Thus the column pointer c should be the column of the new position when updating. We need to move the stacks to the new column before applying the update. So per query:
1. Determine new_h, new_w from current position and d_i.
2. Move column pointer from current_w to new_w (delta -1,0,1) using stack operations.
3. Move row pointer? Row changes by -1,0,1. The row only affects which row in the column is updated; we don't need stacks for rows. Just set current_h = new_h.
4. Update cell (current_h, current_w) from old value to a_i, update ans.
5. Output ans.

This is O(K) per query for the update plus O(K) for the column move (if column changes). Since column changes by at most 1, move is O(K). Total O(Q*K) = ~9e7 operations, feasible in Python if optimized.

Now we need implement the stacks and column transformations.

First, transpose grid if H > W? We want K = min(H,W) as number of rows, L = max(H,W) as number of columns. Paths from (1,1) to (H,W) with moves down/right. If we transpose, moves down/right swap? Let's verify.

Original grid H rows, W cols. If H > W, we can transpose to H'=W, W'=H. A cell (h,w) becomes (w,h). Start (1,1) same, end (H,W) becomes (W,H). Original move down: (h+1,w) -> transposed (w,h+1) = right. Original move right: (h,w+1) -> transposed (w+1,h) = down. So the set of paths from start to end using down/right is preserved. The sum of products is same because each path corresponds bijectively. So we can transpose to ensure rows <= cols. We'll need map the current position and update directions accordingly.

If transposed:
- New H' = W, W' = H.
- Cell (h,w) original -> (w,h) in transposed.
- Start (sh,sw) -> (sw,sh).
- End (H,W) -> (W,H).
- Direction mapping:
  Original D (down, h+1) -> transposed (w, h+1): column increases? Coordinates (row, col) = (w, h+1). Original row h -> transposed col h. So D becomes R (right) in transposed? Let's define transposed row = original col, transposed col = original row.
  Original R (right, w+1) -> transposed (w+1, h): row increases -> D.
  Original U (up, h-1) -> transposed (w, h-1): col decreases -> L.
  Original L (left, w-1) -> transposed (w-1, h): row decreases -> U.
So mapping: D->R, R->D, U->L, L->U. We can just compute new position in transposed coordinates by applying mapped direction.

Alternatively, avoid transposing and set K = min(H,W) by choosing rows = H if H<=W else W? The column transformation method requires rows as the smaller dimension. We can transpose the grid and directions as above.

Now, data structures:

We need to store the grid values in transposed orientation: `A` as list of rows? We need fast access to column values a_i for a given column c and row r. Since K = rows <= 447, L = columns up to 2e5. We can store columns as lists of length K: `cols = [[a_row for row in 1..K] for col in 1..L]`. This is O(N) memory. Access `cols[c][r]` where c 0-index, r 0-index.

We also need to apply column transformation to a vector:
`apply_col(c, X)` returns Y where Y[0]=0; for i in 0..K-1: Y[i] = (a_i * (Y[i-1] if i>0 else 0 + X[i])) % MOD.
This is O(K). We'll call it for moving right (push to left stack) and for computing Y during update maybe.

`apply_col_transpose(c, X)` returns Z = T_c^T X. Recurrence derived:
For T_c upper triangular with entries product from j to i. T_c^T X at index j (0-based row j) = ∑_{i>=j} prod_{k=j..i} a_k * X[i].
Recurrence from bottom:
Z[K-1] = a_{K-1} * X[K-1]? Let's derive 0-based.
Let z_j = a_j * (X[j] + z_{j+1}) for j from K-1 down to 0, with z_K = 0.
Check for j=K-1: z = a_{K-1} X[K-1]. Correct.
For j=K-2: z = a_{K-2}(X[K-2] + z_{K-1}) = a_{K-2}X[K-2] + a_{K-2}a_{K-1}X[K-1]. Correct.
So O(K).

Stacks:
We need store for each stack element the column index and the aggregate vector. But for popping, we only need the previous aggregate vector; we don't need the column index for aggregate, but we need to know which column is being popped to maintain the stack's column set? Actually when moving right, we pop from right stack; the popped column is the current c+1. We don't need to store column index in the stack if we know current c. But for pushing, we need the column index to access its values. We can store the column index along with the aggregate vector, or just store the column index in a separate list. Since stacks partition columns, we can maintain:
- `left_cols`: list of column indices in left stack from bottom to top (1..c-1). But we don't really need the list except to know top column when popping? We know current c, so left top is c-1, right top is c+1. We don't need store column indices at all! The stacks' sizes tell us the columns. For pushing, we know the column index (c when moving right, c when moving left). For popping, we know the column index (c-1 or c+1). So we only need to store aggregate vectors in the stacks.

However, when we pop from left stack, we need to restore the previous aggregate. We can store a list of aggregate vectors where the last element is the current aggregate. Initially, left_stack contains aggregates for prefixes 1..c-1. The length is c-1. The current A = left_stack[-1] if length>0 else e1. When moving right, push new aggregate = apply_col(c, A). When moving left, pop left_stack (if length>0) and A = left_stack[-1] if length>0 else e1. Similarly, right_stack contains aggregates for suffixes c+1..L. The current B = right_stack[-1] if length>0 else e_K. When moving left, push new aggregate = apply_col_transpose(c, B) (since new suffix includes column c). When moving right, pop right_stack and B = right_stack[-1] if length>0 else e_K.

Wait, for right_stack, the aggregate vector stored at top is B_c = (T_L ... T_{c+1})^T e_K. If we push column c when moving left, new B_{c-1} = T_c^T B_c. So push `apply_col_transpose(c, B)`. If we pop when moving right, we remove column c+1; the new B_{c+1} is the aggregate stored below. This works.

Initialization:
- Determine starting column c0 = current position column (transposed).
- Build left_stack:
  A = e1 (vector length K, A[0]=1, rest 0)
  left_stack = []
  for col in 0..c0-2 (0-based columns 1..c0-1): 
      A = apply_col(col, A)
      left_stack.append(A)
  After loop, A is input to column c0 (0-based c0-1). If c0=1, left_stack empty, A=e1.
- Build right_stack:
  B = eK (vector length K, B[K-1]=1, rest 0)
  right_stack = []
  for col in range(L-1, c0-1, -1)? We need columns c0+1..L (0-based c0..L-1? Let's set 0-based columns 0..L-1. Current column index c = current_w - 1. Left stack should contain columns 0..c-1. Right stack should contain columns c+1..L-1. Top of right stack should be c+1.
  To build, start B=eK. For col from L-1 down to c+1: B = apply_col_transpose(col, B); right_stack.append(B). After loop, top is c+1 and B is suffix after c. If c=L-1, right_stack empty, B=eK.
  Note: The order of right_stack list: we append as we go from L-1 down to c+1, so the last appended is c+1, which is top. Good.

Now, current A = left_stack[-1] if left_stack else e1. Current B = right_stack[-1] if right_stack else eK.

We also need the current answer `ans`. We can compute it as:
`mid = apply_col(c, A)` (output of current column)
`ans = dot(B, mid) % MOD`
This is O(K). We can compute once at initialization. Alternatively, compute full DP from start to end O(N) and set ans, but O(K) is fine.

Now, per query:
- Move from current position to new position. We have current row `cur_r` (0-based transposed row), current col `cur_c` (0-based). Direction d_i mapped to transposed direction. Update cur_r, cur_c.
- Adjust stacks to new column:
  while cur_c > current_c: move right
  while cur_c < current_c: move left
  Since delta is at most 1, at most one operation. But if direction is U/D, column unchanged.
  Move right:
    # current column is old c. Push old column onto left stack.
    A = apply_col(old_c, A)
    left_stack.append(A)
    # pop right stack (remove old c+1)
    if right_stack: right_stack.pop()
    B = right_stack[-1] if right_stack else eK
    current_c = old_c + 1
  Move left:
    # push current column? Wait moving left from c to c-1. New suffix should include old column c. We have current A input to old column c, B suffix after old c. Push old column c onto right stack: B = apply_col_transpose(old_c, B); right_stack.append(B). Pop left stack (remove old c-1): if left_stack: left_stack.pop(); A = left_stack[-1] if left_stack else e1. current_c = old_c - 1.
  Let's verify with example. Suppose current c=2 (0-based), left stack columns 0,1, right stack columns 3..L-1. Move left to c=1. New left stack should columns 0 (pop column1). New right stack should columns 2..L-1 (push column2). A_new = input to column1 = aggregate after column0 = left_stack[-2] (pop). B_new = suffix after column1 = (T_L...T_2)^T eK = T_2^T B_old. So push apply_col_transpose(2, B). Correct.
  Move right from c=2 to c=3. New left stack columns 0,1,2 (push column2). A_new = T_2 A_old. New right stack columns 4..L-1 (pop column3). B_new = aggregate below. Correct.

- After moving, current column is cur_c. Now update cell (cur_r, cur_c) from old_val to new_val.

We need compute Δans. We have A = input to current column, B = suffix after current column, and column values `col = cols[cur_c]`.

Compute Y = apply_col(cur_c, A) (output of current column). This is O(K). Actually we might need Y[r-1] for v^T A. We can compute `s = (Y[r-1] if r>0 else 0) + A[r]`. But v^T A = Y[r-1] + A[r]? Let's verify with 0-based r. The recurrence Y[i] = a_i*(Y[i-1]+X[i]). For changing a_r (0-based), s = Y[r-1] + X[r] (with Y[-1]=0). Yes.

Compute `dot_Bu`: u vector depends on column values after r. We can compute iteratively:
u = 0 for i<r, u[r]=1, for i>r u[i] = u[i-1]*a_i.
Then dot_Bu = sum_{i=r}^{K-1} B[i] * u[i].
We can compute in O(K) by iterating i from r to K-1:
  val = 1
  for i in range(r, K):
      dot_Bu += B[i] * val
      if i+1 < K: val = val * col[i+1] % MOD
But careful: u[i] = product_{k=r+1..i} a_k for i>r, u[r]=1. So for i=r, val=1. For i=r+1, val *= a_{r+1}. Yes.

`dot_vA`: v vector: for j<r, v[j] = product_{k=j..r-1} a_k; v[r]=1; j>r 0. We can compute by iterating j from r down to 0:
  val = 1
  for j in range(r, -1, -1):
      dot_vA += A[j] * val
      if j>0: val = val * col[j-1]? Wait v[j-1] = a_{j-1} * v[j]? Let's derive.
v[r] = 1.
v[r-1] = a_{r-1} (product k=r-1..r-1).
v[r-2] = a_{r-2} * a_{r-1} = a_{r-2} * v[r-1].
So going downward: for j from r down to 0:
  dot_vA += A[j] * val
  if j>0: val = val * col[j-1]  # because v[j-1] = col[j-1] * v[j]
Check: at j=r, val=1, add A[r]. Then val = col[r-1]*1 = v[r-1]. At j=r-1, add A[r-1]*v[r-1]. Then val = col[r-2]*v[r-1] = v[r-2]. Correct.

Then `delta_ans = delta * dot_Bu % MOD * dot_vA % MOD`.
`ans = (ans + delta_ans) % MOD`.

But wait, does this formula use B_c^T u * v^T A_c? Yes.

We also need update the column value: `col[r] = new_val`. This changes T_c. The stacks' A and B do not include T_c, so they remain valid. The current answer ans updated. The `Y` we computed is old output; no need to store.

Edge cases:
- If delta = 0, ans unchanged, but still update col value (same).
- If r=0: v^T A = A[0] (since Y[-1]=0). Our loop handles.
- If r=K-1: u only at r, dot_Bu = B[r]. Loop handles.

Now, after update, output ans.

We must ensure the initial ans is correct. We can compute by applying all columns? Our stack initialization gives A input to current column and B suffix after current. `ans = dot(B, apply_col(cur_c, A))` gives total answer. Let's test with sample.

Let's manually test small grid H=2,W=3 sample. H<=W so no transpose. K=2,L=3.
Initial grid:
col1: [1,4], col2:[2,5], col3:[3,6].

Now we need handle row moves and transposition.

Let's implement transposition and direction mapping.

Input:
H W
A matrix H rows W cols.
Q sh sw
Then Q lines d_i a_i.

If H <= W:
  K = H, L = W.
  cols = list of columns: for c in 0..W-1, col = [A[r][c] for r in 0..H-1].
  start_r = sh-1, start_c = sw-1.
  Direction mapping: original directions in transposed? No transpose, so:
    U: r -= 1
    D: r += 1
    L: c -= 1
    R: c += 1
If H > W:
  K = W, L = H.
  Transposed grid rows = original cols, cols = original rows.
  cols = list of columns in transposed: for c in 0..H-1 (original row index), col = [A[c][r] for r in 0..W-1]? Let's define transposed cell (row = original col, col = original row). So for transposed column index c (0..H-1) corresponding to original row, the K rows are original columns 0..W-1. Thus col = [A[original_row][orig_col] for orig_col in 0..W-1].
  start_r = sw-1 (original col), start_c = sh-1 (original row).
  Direction mapping:
    Original D (h+1) -> transposed col increases (c+1) -> R
    Original R (w+1) -> transposed row increases (r+1) -> D
    Original U (h-1) -> transposed col decreases (c-1) -> L
    Original L (w-1) -> transposed row decreases (r-1) -> U
  So in processing, given d:
    if d == 'D': c += 1
    elif d == 'R': r += 1
    elif d == 'U': c -= 1
    elif d == 'L': r -= 1
  Wait for H>W, transposed row = original col, transposed col = original row. Original D increases original row -> transposed col increases. Yes.
  Original R increases original col -> transposed row increases. Yes.
  Original U decreases original row -> transposed col decreases.
  Original L decreases original col -> transposed row decreases.

We can unify by mapping to (dr, dc) in transposed:
if not transposed:
  U:(-1,0), D:(1,0), L:(0,-1), R:(0,1)
else:
  U:(0,-1), D:(0,1), L:(-1,0), R:(1,0)

We'll read all queries into a list to process? We can process online, but we need to know initial grid. We can process as we read. Since we need stacks initialized before queries, we can read Q, sh, sw, then process lines one by one. We don't need store all queries. However, the input format has Q sh sw on one line, then Q lines. We can read all tokens via sys.stdin.buffer.read().split() for speed.

Implementation details for performance:
- K <= 447, L <= 2e5, Q <= 2e5. O(Q*K) ~9e7. Python loops over K per query; need optimize.

We'll represent vectors as Python lists of ints length K. Applying column transformation:
```
def apply_col(c, X):
    a = cols[c]
    Y = [0]*K
    prev = 0
    for i in range(K):
        prev = (a[i] * (prev + X[i])) % MOD
        Y[i] = prev
    return Y
```
This allocates a new list each time. We call apply_col for each move right and for computing Y during update. Move left uses apply_col_transpose. Per query, we call at most one apply_col/transpose for move plus one apply_col for update (to compute Y). So ~2*K operations per query. 2*9e7=180M modular multiplications, maybe high but possibly okay in Py if optimized? Need reduce.

We can avoid computing Y during update by computing dot_vA directly from A and column values (O(K)) and dot_Bu O(K). We don't need full Y. The formula dot_vA = v^T A can be computed with the downward loop using A and col, no need Y. Wait v^T A = Y[r-1] + A[r], but the loop we wrote computes v^T A directly without Y. Yes! We don't need apply_col for update. Let's verify: dot_vA loop:
val=1; for j=r..0: dot += A[j]*val; if j>0: val = val*col[j-1].
This computes ∑_{j=0}^r A[j] * v[j] where v[j]=prod_{k=j}^{r-1} a_k for j<r, v[r]=1. This equals Y[r-1]+A[r]? For r=1, dot = A[1] + A[0]*a0. Y[0]=a0*A[0], so Y[0]+A[1]=a0*A[0]+A[1]. Yes. So no apply_col for update. Great.

For moving right, we need apply_col to compute new A. For moving left, apply_col_transpose to compute new B. So per query at most one apply_col/transpose for move, plus O(K) for update. If column doesn't change, no apply. Total ~Q*K operations for moves + Q*K for update = 2QK ~9e7? Actually move apply is K, update dot_Bu and dot_vA each K, so 3K per query. 3*9e7=270M. Still high but maybe okay with simple loops and small K? Need optimize.

We can combine dot_Bu and dot_vA in one loop? They are independent. We can compute both in one pass over K:
- dot_vA needs downward from r to 0.
- dot_Bu needs upward from r to K-1.
We can do two loops. K<=447, Q=2e5 => 89M iterations per loop, 178M total. Plus moves. Python 178M modular multiplications might be ~10-20 seconds, maybe too high. But perhaps K worst only when L~447, Q=2e5, and many moves? Still.

We can optimize by using local variables, avoid modulo in every multiplication? Values <MOD, product <1e18, Python int can handle. We can accumulate and modulo at end? dot products of up to 447 terms each <MOD, sum < 447*1e18 ~4e20, Python big int but okay. We can do `dot = (dot + B[i] * val) % MOD` each iteration to keep small. Mod operation is expensive. We can accumulate in Python int and take `% MOD` at the end; 447 terms of ~1e18 sum ~4e20, which is 69 bits, still small (Python int 2-3 limbs). So we can avoid modulo inside loops for dot products! For val (product of column values), we need modulo to prevent exponential growth. val = val * col[i] % MOD each step. So one modulo per step for val. dot addition no modulo until end. This reduces cost.

For apply_col, recurrence prev = a[i]*(prev+X[i]) % MOD requires modulo each step. That's K per move. Move occurs every query? The walk moves one cell each query, so column changes by -1,0,1. If moves are vertical, no apply. Worst horizontal moves every query, so Q apply_col/transpose. Each K modulo. 9e7 modulo operations, okay maybe.

Can we avoid storing Y list in apply_col? We only need the final vector to push. We can compute new vector in a preallocated list? But we need to store it in stack. We can create a list of length K each time. Allocation overhead. We can use a list comprehension? Recurrence is sequential, not easily.

We can implement apply_col in-place on a copy? For moving right, we need old A to compute new A; we can create new list. For moving left, similar. Allocation of 447-length list 2e5 times = 89M elements allocated, okay but overhead.

Stack storage: left_stack and right_stack store aggregate vectors. Total vectors L-1 <=2e5, each length K, total N=2e5 ints. But as lists, each vector is a separate list object; L up to 2e5, K=2, many small lists overhead huge. However, total number of vectors across stacks is L-1, which can be 2e5 when K=2. Each vector length 2, list overhead ~56 bytes => 11MB, okay. When K=447, L=447, vectors 446, each 447 ints => ~200k ints, okay. So memory fine.

But we also store `cols` as L lists of length K, total N ints. Fine.

We need to initialize stacks by pushing L-1 vectors, O(N) time. Good.

Let's test the stack operations with 0-based indexing carefully.

Variables:
- `cur_c`: current column index 0..L-1.
- `left_stack`: list of aggregate vectors for prefixes ending at columns 0..cur_c-1. `left_stack[-1]` is A (input to cur_c). If empty, A = e1.
- `right_stack`: list of aggregate vectors for suffixes starting at columns cur_c+1..L-1. `right_stack[-1]` is B (suffix after cur_c). If empty, B = eK.

Initialization:
```
A = [0]*K; A[0]=1
left_stack=[]
for col in range(0, cur_c):
    A = apply_col(col, A)
    left_stack.append(A)
```
After loop, left_stack length = cur_c. If cur_c=0, A=e1.

```

    right_stack.append(B)
```
After loop, right_stack length = L-1-cur_c. Top is cur_c+1. If cur_c=L-1, B=eK.

Move right from cur_c to cur_c+1:
```

left_stack.append(A)
# pop right_stack (top is cur_c+1)

    right_stack.pop()

```
But wait, after pushing old cur_c to left, A_new = T_cur_c A_old. This is input to cur_c+1. Correct. The right_stack top before pop is suffix after cur_c (columns cur_c+1..). After moving to cur_c+1, suffix should be columns cur_c+2.., so pop top (cur_c+1). The new B is the aggregate below. Correct.

Move left from cur_c to cur_c-1:
```

    left_stack.pop()

Here old cur_c is the column we are leaving; new suffix after cur_c-1 includes old cur_c, so B_new = T_cur_c^T B_old. Correct. New left stack should remove old cur_c-1, so pop. A_new = input to cur_c-1. Correct.

Now, update formula with 0-based r:
- `col = cols[cur_c]`
- `old = col[r]`, `new = a_i`, `delta = new - old` (mod MOD; delta can be negative, use `% MOD`? We can compute delta = (new - old) % MOD, and ans = (ans + delta * ...) % MOD. Since modulo arithmetic, delta can be negative but Python % handles. Use `delta = new - old` and later `% MOD` at end; product may be negative, but ans %= MOD. To keep positive, `delta %= MOD` maybe. We'll do `delta = (new - old) % MOD`.

Compute dot_Bu:
```
dotBu = 0
val = 1
for i in range(r, K):
    dotBu += B[i] * val
    if i + 1 < K:
        val = (val * col[i+1]) % MOD
```
But note u[i] for i>r uses product of a_{r+1..i}. In loop, at i=r, val=1. After processing i, before next i+1, val *= col[i+1] (which is a_{i+1}). So at i=r+1, val = a_{r+1}. Correct.

Compute dot_vA:
```
dotvA = 0
val = 1
for j in range(r, -1, -1):
    dotvA += A[j] * val
    if j > 0:
        val = (val * col[j-1]) % MOD
```
At j=r, val=1. After, val *= col[r-1] = v[r-1]. At j=r-1, add A[r-1]*v[r-1]. Correct.

Then:
```
delta_ans = delta * (dotBu % MOD) % MOD * (dotvA % MOD) % MOD
ans = (ans + delta_ans) % MOD
col[r] = new
```

We should also consider if old == new, delta=0, but still update col (same). Fine.

We can compute with a loop.

Now, handling modulo for column values and vectors. All values stored modulo MOD. apply_col uses modulo.

Potential issue: The rank-1 update formula assumes the column transformation T_c is defined with current column values. When we update `col[r]`, the stacks' aggregate vectors A and B do not include T_c, so they remain correct. But what about `ans`? We updated it. What about the `mid` vector (T_c A) if needed later? We don't store it. When we later move right, we push T_c A using apply_col with updated col. Good.

When moving left, we push T_c^T B using apply_col_transpose with updated col. Good.

Now, we must ensure that when we move, the stacks' stored aggregate vectors for columns that were previously pushed remain correct even if those columns are later updated? Wait, a column can be updated multiple times. If a column is currently in the left_stack (i.e., to the left of current position), and later we move right past it? Actually left_stack contains columns < current_c. If we update a column, it is the current column, which is not in left_stack or right_stack. So stacks don't contain the current column. If we later move right, we push the current column (with updated values) onto left_stack. If we later move left back to it, we pop it from left_stack? Let's see: After moving right, the old current column is in left_stack. If we later move left, we pop it from left_stack and it becomes current again. Its aggregate vector in left_stack was computed with the values at the time it was pushed. If the column is not current, it cannot be updated (updates only happen at current position). So its values don't change while in the stack. Good. Similarly, right_stack columns are to the right and not updated until we move to them; when we move right, we pop from right_stack and make it current, then update. So stack aggregates remain valid.

What about a column that is in right_stack and later we move left? It stays in right_stack; not updated. Good.

Thus the stack approach is correct.

Now, complexity and memory.

Let's implement `apply_col` and `apply_col_transpose` as nested functions using local variables for speed. But function call overhead per move/update? apply_col called at most once per query; update loops inline. We can define functions.

```
def apply_col(c, X):
    a = cols[c]
    Y = [0]*K
    prev = 0
    mod = MOD
    for i in range(K):
        prev = (a[i] * (prev + X[i])) % mod
        Y[i] = prev
    return Y
```

`apply_col_transpose`:
```
def apply_col_transpose(c, X):
    a = cols[c]
    Y = [0]*K
    nxt = 0
    mod = MOD
    for i in range(K-1, -1, -1):
        nxt = (a[i] * (X[i] + nxt)) % mod
        Y[i] = nxt
    return Y
```
Check: z_j = a_j*(X[j] + z_{j+1}). We iterate i from K-1 down to 0, `nxt` is z_{i+1}. At i=K-1, nxt=0, z= a[K-1]*X[K-1]. Good.

Initialization loops:
```
A = [0]*K; A[0]=1
left_stack=[]
for col in range(cur_c):
    A = apply_col(col, A)
    left_stack.append(A)
```
This stores A after each prefix. Note that `A` is a list; when we append and then later reassign A, the appended list is not mutated. apply_col creates a new list, so safe.

Right:
```
B = [0]*K; B[K-1]=1
right_stack=[]
for col in range(L-1, cur_c, -1):
    B = apply_col_transpose(col, B)
    right_stack.append(B)
```

Initial ans:
```
mid = apply_col(cur_c, A)
ans = 0
for i in range(K):
    ans += B[i] * mid[i]
ans %= MOD
```
