Now, initial `cur_c` might be 0 or L-1; stacks built accordingly.

Let's consider if K=1? Not possible but if H=1? Constraints H,W>=2, so K>=2. But after transpose, K>=2. Good.

Now, modulo for negative delta: We'll do `delta = (new_val - old_val) % MOD`. Then `delta_ans = delta * dotBu % MOD * dotvA % MOD`. `dotBu` and `dotvA` are Python ints possibly large; take `% MOD` before multiplication to keep small:
```
delta_ans = delta * (dotBu % MOD) % MOD * (dotvA % MOD) % MOD
ans = (ans + delta_ans) % MOD
```
`dotBu` and `dotvA` can be computed without modulo inside, but val uses modulo. At the end `% MOD`.

In dot loops, `B[i]` and `A[j]` are modulo values. `val` modulo. The sum of 447 terms each <MOD^2 ~1e18, sum ~4e20, Python int 69 bits, fine.

In apply_col, `prev + X[i]` < 2*MOD, times a[i] < 1e18, `% MOD`. Good.

Now, performance of `apply_col` and `apply_col_transpose` with list allocation. We can optimize by using local variables:
```
def apply_col(c, X, cols=cols, K=K, MOD=MOD):
    a = cols[c]
    Y = [0]*K
    prev = 0
    for i in range(K):
        prev = (a[i] * (prev + X[i])) % MOD
        Y[i] = prev
    return Y
```
But `cols` and `K` are defined after; we can define functions after building cols.

We might inline apply_col in the move code to avoid function call overhead? Function call per query is okay (2e5). But inside function, loop K. We'll keep functions for clarity.

However, `apply_col_transpose` uses `range(K-1, -1, -1)`, which creates a range object each call. Fine.

Let's think about memory of `left_stack` and `right_stack`. We store aggregate vectors for every column. When we move right, we append to left_stack; when we move left, we pop from left_stack and append to right_stack. The total number of vectors in both stacks remains L-1 (except current column not stored). But wait, when we move right, we append to left_stack and pop from right_stack, so total unchanged. When move left, append to right_stack and pop left_stack, total unchanged. So memory O(L*K). Good.

But we also store the aggregate vectors in the stacks as lists. When we pop, the list is removed but Python may keep memory; fine.

Now, let's verify the initial answer computation with transposition. The answer should be same. Our column transformations assume paths from top-left to bottom-right in transposed grid. Transposition preserves path set, so yes.

Let's test sample 2? We can't run, but trust.

Potential issue: The initial vector for columns is e1 = [1,0,...,0]. This represents the boundary before column 1. For the first column, recurrence Y[1] = A[1,1]*(Y[0]+X[1]) with X[1]=1, so F[1,1]=A[1,1]. Good. For rows >1 in first column, X[i]=0, so F[i,1] = A[i,1]*F[i-1,1], correct. The final answer after all columns is state at row K (bottom). We use eK = [0,...,1] for suffix. Good.

Now, what about the start cell if it is updated? The update formula handles any cell, including (1,1). If updating (1,1) in column 0, r=0. A = e1, B = suffix after col0. Δans = delta * (B^T u) * (v^T A). v^T A = A[0]=1. u = [1, a2, a2*a3,...] for column 0. This gives change in total answer due to changing start value. Correct.

What if updating a cell with old value 0? The rank-1 formula still works because we use delta and column values for u/v; no division. Good.

Now, let's ensure the column transformation T_c and rank-1 update formula are correct for changing a_r by delta. We derived ΔY = u * (Y[r-1]+X[r]). But in the rank-1 form ΔT_c = u v^T, v^T X = Y[r-1]+X[r]. We computed dot_vA directly as ∑_{j=0}^r A[j] v[j]. Let's verify with a random small example by hand.

K=2, col a=[a0,a1], X=[x0,x1]. Y0=a0*x0. Y1=a1*(Y0+x1)=a1*a0*x0 + a1*x1.
Change a0 by d:
Y0' = (a0+d)x0 = Y0 + d*x0.
Y1' = a1*(Y0'+x1) = a1*(a0*x0 + d*x0 + x1) = Y1 + d*a1*x0.
So ΔY = [d*x0, d*a1*x0].
Our u for r=0: u=[1, a1]. v for r=0: v=[1,0]. v^T X = x0. ΔT = u v^T = [[1,0],[a1,0]]. ΔT X = [x0, a1*x0]. Times d. Correct.
dot_vA = A[0] = x0. dot_Bu = B0*1 + B1*a1. Δans = d*x0*(B0+B1*a1). If B is suffix after col, answer change should d*x0*(B0 + a1*B1). Yes.

Change a1 by d:
Y0 unchanged. Y1' = (a1+d)*(Y0+x1) = Y1 + d*(Y0+x1).
u for r=1: u=[0,1]. v for r=1: v=[a0,1]. v^T X = a0*x0 + x1 = Y0+x1. ΔT = u v^T = [[0,0],[a0,1]]. ΔT X = [0, a0*x0+x1]. Correct.
dot_vA loop: j=1 val=1 add A1; val*=col[0]=a0; j=0 add A0*a0. Good.

Now, apply_col_transpose correctness for T_c^T:
T_c matrix rows i, cols j: T[i][j] = prod_{k=j..i} a_k for i>=j. T^T vector z_j = ∑_{i>=j} T[i][j] X[i]. Recurrence z_j = a_j*(X[j]+z_{j+1}). We implemented.

Now, one more subtlety: The stacks store aggregate vectors A and B modulo MOD. When we update a column, the rank-1 update to ans uses B and A which are modulo values. Since all operations are linear over the field, modulo works.

Now, let's consider the initial building of right_stack. We start B=eK and for col from L-1 down to cur_c+1: B = apply_col_transpose(col, B); right_stack.append(B). This means right_stack[0] is aggregate for suffix starting at L-1? Let's see: L=3, cur_c=1. Loop col=2: B=T2^T eK, append. right_stack=[B_suffix_col2]. Top is col2. Good. If cur_c=0, loop col=2,1: after col2 append B_suf2; after col1 B=T1^T B_suf2 = suffix after col0 (cols1,2), append. right_stack=[B_suf2, B_suf0? actually top is col1]. The aggregate at top is suffix after col0. When moving right to col1, we pop top, B becomes B_suf2 (suffix after col1). Correct.

Left_stack building: L=3, cur_c=1. Loop col=0: A=T0 e1, append. left_stack=[A_col0]. Top is col0, input to col1. When moving left to col0, pop, A=e1. Correct.

Now, after moving right, we push old current column onto left_stack. Suppose current c=1, left_stack=[A_col0], right_stack=[B_suf2]. Move right to c=2:
A = apply_col(1, A) = T1 A_col0 = input to col2. left_stack.append(A) => [A_col0, A_col1].
pop right_stack -> empty, B=eK. Now current c=2, A input to col2, B suffix after col2. Correct.

Now, update at current column after moving. Good.

Let's think about the answer after moving but before update: We don't need to update ans when moving; ans remains the total answer for the current grid, independent of position. The stacks A/B are just for computation; ans is global. When we move, we adjust A/B to the new column but ans stays. Then update changes ans. Good.

Now, what if the move changes column but the current column's transformation T_c was updated earlier and is in the stack? We push/pop using updated values. Good.

d = next(it)

```

Direction mapping with transposition:
If not transposed:
```
if d == b'U': cur_r -= 1
elif d == b'D': cur_r += 1
elif d == b'L': cur_c -= 1
else: cur_c += 1
```
If transposed:
```
if d == b'U': cur_c -= 1
elif d == b'D': cur_c += 1
elif d == b'L': cur_r -= 1
else: cur_r += 1
```
We can set a flag `transposed`.

Let's consider the modulo value of `delta = (new_val - old_val) % MOD`. If old_val and new_val are already modded, delta is modded. The rank-1 formula over field: Δans = (new-old)*... mod MOD. Using delta mod MOD is correct.

Now, let's think about the column transformation T_c when a column has zeros. The rank-1 update formula still works because we never divide. apply_col/transpose handle zeros. The stacks' aggregate vectors may become zero but that's fine.

Now, one more subtlety: The initial vector e1 for the left boundary assumes the cell (1,1) is the start. If we transpose, start is still (1,1) in transposed coordinates? Original start (sh,sw) maps to (sw,sh). The top-left (1,1) remains (1,1). The initial vector e1 is for row 1, column 0 boundary. This is correct regardless of start position; the DP always starts at (1,1). The current position is just where updates happen; the DP boundary is fixed.

Potential optimization: In the update dot loops, `range(r, K)` and `range(r, -1, -1)` allocate range objects; fine. We can use while loops for speed? For loops in Python are okay. But 2e5*447 ~89M iterations per loop; two loops ~178M. For loops with range are optimized. We'll keep.

We can reduce dot loops by noting if delta==0, skip computing dotBu/dotvA and just update col (same). But if delta=0, no answer change; we can skip loops. Add:
```
if delta:
    compute...
else:
    col[r] = new
```
But new==old, col unchanged. We can just continue? Need still output ans. We can skip update entirely. However, if new==old, no change. We can `if delta == 0: output ans; continue` after moving stacks. But we still need to update col? It's same, no. So skip. This saves time for repeated same values.

Also, if K is small, loops cheap.

We can precompute `e1` and `eK` as tuples? Lists.

Let's implement `apply_col` and `apply_col_transpose` using local `MOD` and `K`.

```
def apply_col(c, X):
    a = cols[c]
    Y = [0] * K
    prev = 0
    mod = MOD
    for i in range(K):
        prev = (a[i] * (prev + X[i])) % mod
        Y[i] = prev
    return Y
```

`apply_col_transpose`:
```
def apply_col_transpose(c, X):
    a = cols[c]
    Y = [0] * K
    nxt = 0
    mod = MOD
    for i in range(K-1, -1, -1):
        nxt = (a[i] * (X[i] + nxt)) % mod
        Y[i] = nxt
    return Y
```

In initialization, building left_stack by calling apply_col for each column up to cur_c. If cur_c large (e.g., 1e5, K=2), 2e5 calls, each loop K=2, fine. Total O(N).

Now, reading input: We need to read Q, sh, sw after grid. The input format:
```
H W
A...
Q sh sw
d1 a1
...
```
So after grid, there is a line with Q sh sw. We'll parse.

Let's code:

```
def solve():
    import sys
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    it = iter(data)
    H = int(next(it)); W = int(next(it))
    grid = []
    for _ in range(H):
        row = [int(next(it)) % MOD for _ in range(W)]
        grid.append(row)
    Q = int(next(it)); sh = int(next(it)); sw = int(next(it))
    ...
```
Wait, after grid, the next token is Q, then sh, sw. Yes.

But if we build cols before reading Q? We need Q to know? No. We can build cols after reading grid and before Q? The tokens order: after grid, Q sh sw. We can read Q after building cols? The iterator is positioned after grid; we can build cols using grid, then read Q. But building cols doesn't consume tokens. So:
- Read grid.
- Build cols and set transposed.
- Read Q, sh, sw from iterator.
This is fine because iterator is independent.

But we need to know transposed to map start. We can build cols before reading Q. Good.

Let's build cols:
```
if H <= W:
    transposed = False
    K, L = H, W
    cols = [[grid[r][c] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]
    start_r = sh - 1
    start_c = sw - 1
else:
    transposed = True
    K, L = W, H
    cols = [[grid[c][r] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]
    start_r = sw - 1
    start_c = sh - 1
```
But `sh, sw` are read after grid, so we need read them before setting start. We can read Q, sh, sw before building cols? Actually we need sh/sw to set start, but cols building doesn't need sh/sw. We can read Q/sh/sw right after grid, then build cols. Let's do:
```
Q = int(next(it)); sh = int(next(it)); sw = int(next(it))
# then build cols using grid and sh/sw
```
But the iterator after reading Q/sh/sw is at first query. Building cols doesn't consume iterator. Fine.

So:
```
Q = int(next(it)); sh = int(next(it)); sw = int(next(it))
if H <= W: ...
```

Now, direction mapping. We'll use if/elif in query loop.

Initialize stacks:
```
e1 = [0]*K; e1[0]=1
eK = [0]*K; eK[K-1]=1

A = e1[:]  # but e1 won't be mutated? apply_col reads X, doesn't mutate. We can use e1 directly as A? When moving, A = apply_col(...), so A rebinds. We should not mutate A. Use e1.
left_stack = []
for col in range(cur_c):
    A = apply_col(col, A)
    left_stack.append(A)
```
If cur_c=0, A=e1. Note: `A` initially e1; if we append e1? No, we only append after apply_col. If cur_c=0, left_stack empty, A=e1. Good.

Right:
```
B = eK[:]  # eK not mutated
right_stack = []
for col in range(L-1, cur_c, -1):
    B = apply_col_transpose(col, B)
    right_stack.append(B)
```

Initial ans:
```
mid = apply_col(cur_c, A)
ans = 0
for i in range(K):
    ans += B[i] * mid[i]
ans %= MOD
```

Now, process queries:
```
out = []
for _ in range(Q):
    d = next(it)
    a = int(next(it)) % MOD
    old_r = cur_r; old_c = cur_c
    if not transposed:
        if d == b'U':
            cur_r -= 1
        elif d == b'D':
            cur_r += 1
        elif d == b'L':
            cur_c -= 1
        else: # R
            cur_c += 1
    else:
        if d == b'U':
            cur_c -= 1
        elif d == b'D':
            cur_c += 1
        elif d == b'L':
            cur_r -= 1
        else: # R
            cur_r += 1

    # adjust stacks from old_c to cur_c
    if cur_c > old_c:
        # move right
        A = apply_col(old_c, A)
        left_stack.append(A)
        if right_stack:
            right_stack.pop()
        B = right_stack[-1] if right_stack else eK
    elif cur_c < old_c:
        # move left
        B = apply_col_transpose(old_c, B)
        right_stack.append(B)
        if left_stack:
            left_stack.pop()
        A = left_stack[-1] if left_stack else e1

    # update cell
    col = cols[cur_c]
    old_val = col[cur_r]
    delta = (a - old_val) % MOD
    if delta:
        # compute dotBu
        dotBu = 0
        val = 1
        # local references for speed
        Bv = B; Av = A; colv = col; mod = MOD
        for i in range(cur_r, K):
            dotBu += Bv[i] * val
            if i + 1 < K:
                val = (val * colv[i+1]) % mod
        dotvA = 0
        val = 1
        for j in range(cur_r, -1, -1):
            dotvA += Av[j] * val
            if j > 0:
                val = (val * colv[j-1]) % mod
        delta_ans = delta * (dotBu % mod) % mod * (dotvA % mod) % mod
        ans = (ans + delta_ans) % mod
        col[cur_r] = a
    else:
        col[cur_r] = a  # same, but ensure
    out.append(str(ans))
sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
```

Wait, in the move right, we set `B = right_stack[-1] if right_stack else eK`. But `eK` is a list; if we later use B in apply_col_transpose when moving left, it reads B, doesn't mutate. Good. But if we assign B = eK, and later `B = apply_col_transpose(old_c, B)`, B rebinds. eK remains. Good.

Similarly, `A = e1` if left_stack empty.

One issue: In initialization, `A = e1` and `left_stack` may be empty. If we later move left and pop left_stack, `A = left_stack[-1] if left_stack else e1`. Good.

Now, in the update delta computation, `dotBu` and `dotvA` can be large; we take `% mod` at the end. But `delta_ans = delta * (dotBu % mod) % mod * (dotvA % mod) % mod` is correct. `ans = (ans + delta_ans) % mod`.

We should update `col[cur_r] = a` even if delta=0? It's same, but do it.

Now, let's write the final code with `MOD = 998244353`.

Let's double-check the update formula with `dotBu` and `dotvA` when `cur_r` is not the last row. We used `if i + 1 < K` inside loop to update val. This branch inside loop may slow. We can avoid by looping to K-1 for val update separately:
```
dotBu = 0
val = 1
for i in range(r, K):
    dotBu += B[i] * val
    val = (val * col[i+1]) % MOD if i+1 < K else 0
```
The branch is okay. We can also split:
```
for i in range(r, K-1):
    dotBu += B[i] * val
    val = val * col[i+1] % MOD
dotBu += B[K-1] * val
```
This avoids branch. Similarly for dotvA:
```
dotvA = 0
val = 1
for j in range(r, 0, -1):
    dotvA += A[j] * val
    val = val * col[j-1] % MOD
dotvA += A[0] * val
```
This is faster. Let's use this.

For dotBu:
```
dotBu = 0
val = 1
for i in range(r, K-1):
    dotBu += B[i] * val
    val = (val * col[i+1]) % MOD
dotBu += B[K-1] * val
```
If r == K-1, range(r,K-1) empty, dotBu = B[K-1]*1. Correct.

For dotvA:
```
dotvA = 0
val = 1
for j in range(r, 0, -1):
    dotvA += A[j] * val
    val = (val * col[j-1]) % MOD
dotvA += A[0] * val
```
If r==0, range empty, dotvA = A[0]*1. Correct.

Now, in the update, we use `mod = MOD` local. The dot products accumulate without modulo; val modulo. At the end `dotBu % mod`.

```
def solve():
    import sys
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    it = iter(data)
    H = int(next(it)); W = int(next(it))
    grid = []
    for _ in range(H):
        row = [int(next(it)) % MOD for _ in range(W)]
        grid.append(row)
    Q = int(next(it)); sh = int(next(it)); sw = int(next(it))

    if H <= W:
        transposed = False
        K, L = H, W
        cols = [[grid[r][c] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]
        cur_r = sh - 1
        cur_c = sw - 1
    else:
        transposed = True
        K, L = W, H
        cols = [[grid[c][r] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]
        cur_r = sw - 1
        cur_c = sh - 1
    del grid
    ...

Wait, if H>W, `cols = [[grid[c][r] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]` where L=H, K=W. This iterates c over original rows, r over original cols. Good.

Now, let's think about the case where the first move is from start to a cell, and the start cell might be updated later. Our stacks are built for the initial grid. When we move away from start, start column may be pushed into left/right stack. If start is later updated, we will move back to it, pop it from stack, update, and push again. The stack aggregate for start when it was pushed was computed with old value; but when we pop it, we discard that aggregate. Then after updating, if we move away, we push with new value. So no stale aggregates. Good.

Now, let's consider if a column in the right_stack is updated? To update a column, it must become current. To become current, we move left into it, which pops it from right_stack? Wait, moving left from c to c-1: we push old c onto right_stack and pop left_stack. The new current c-1 was previously in left_stack (top), not right_stack. So moving left does not pop right_stack; it pushes onto right_stack. Moving right pops right_stack. Thus a column in right_stack becomes current when we move right into it (pop from right_stack). A column in left_stack becomes current when we move left into it (pop from left_stack). So any column that is updated is popped from a stack (or is the initial current column). Its aggregate in the stack is removed before update. After update, if we move away, it is pushed with new values. Thus no stale aggregates. Good.

Now, let's ensure the total number of vectors in stacks remains L-1. When moving right, left_stack size +1, right_stack size -1. When moving left, left_stack -1, right_stack +1. So sum constant. Initial sum = cur_c + (L-1-cur_c) = L-1. Good.

Now, let's think about the initial `right_stack` building order. We append B for each col from L-1 down to cur_c+1. The list order is [B_suf_L-1, B_suf_L-2, ..., B_suf_cur_c+1]. The top is last element B_suf_cur_c+1. When moving right, we pop top, which is B_suf_cur_c+1, leaving B_suf_cur_c+2 as new top. This matches suffix after cur_c+1. Good.

Now, let's consider if `right_stack` is empty and we move right? That would mean cur_c = L-1, cannot. If `left_stack` empty and move left? cur_c=0, cannot. So safe.

Now, let's think about the update formula if the current column transformation T_c has changed since A or B were computed? A and B are input/suffix, independent of T_c. They were computed using columns before/after, not current. So even if T_c changed earlier? Wait, T_c is the current column; it may have been updated before when it was current. A and B do not include T_c, so they are unaffected by T_c's value. Good.

Now, let's consider if the current column was previously in a stack and then became current by popping; its aggregate in the stack was removed. A and B after popping are the aggregates for the new current column. The popped column's values are the current column values (which may have been updated when it was current before, and not changed while in stack). So T_c is up to date. Good.

Now, let's think about the initial answer if the grid has zeros and modulo. The DP values are modulo; the rank-1 update formula is linear over modulo, so correct.

Full code:

```
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    it = iter(data)
    H = int(next(it)); W = int(next(it))
    grid = []
    for _ in range(H):
        row = [int(next(it)) % MOD for _ in range(W)]
        grid.append(row)
    Q = int(next(it)); sh = int(next(it)); sw = int(next(it))

    if H <= W:
        transposed = False
        K, L = H, W
        cols = [[grid[r][c] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]
        cur_r = sh - 1
        cur_c = sw - 1
    else:
        transposed = True
        K, L = W, H
        cols = [[grid[c][r] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]
        cur_r = sw - 1
        cur_c = sh - 1
    del grid

    def apply_col(c, X):
        a = cols[c]
        Y = [0] * K
        prev = 0
        mod = MOD
        for i in range(K):
            prev = (a[i] * (prev + X[i])) % mod
            Y[i] = prev
        return Y

    def apply_col_transpose(c, X):
        a = cols[c]
        Y = [0] * K
        nxt = 0
        mod = MOD
        for i in range(K-1, -1, -1):
            nxt = (a[i] * (X[i] + nxt)) % mod
            Y[i] = nxt
        return Y

    e1 = [0] * K
    e1[0] = 1
    eK = [0] * K
    eK[K-1] = 1

    A = e1
    left_stack = []
    for col in range(cur_c):
        A = apply_col(col, A)
        left_stack.append(A)

    B = eK
    right_stack = []
    for col in range(L-1, cur_c, -1):
        B = apply_col_transpose(col, B)
        right_stack.append(B)

    mid = apply_col(cur_c, A)
    ans = 0
    for i in range(K):
        ans += B[i] * mid[i]
    ans %= MOD

    out = []
    mod = MOD
    for _ in range(Q):
        d = next(it)
        a = int(next(it)) % mod

        old_c = cur_c
        if not transposed:
            if d == b'U':
                cur_r -= 1
            elif d == b'D':
                cur_r += 1
            elif d == b'L':
                cur_c -= 1
            else:  # R
                cur_c += 1
        else:
            if d == b'U':
                cur_c -= 1
            elif d == b'D':
                cur_c += 1
            elif d == b'L':
                cur_r -= 1
            else:  # R
                cur_r += 1

        if cur_c > old_c:
            A = apply_col(old_c, A)
            left_stack.append(A)
            if right_stack:
                right_stack.pop()
            B = right_stack[-1] if right_stack else eK
        elif cur_c < old_c:
            B = apply_col_transpose(old_c, B)
            right_stack.append(B)
            if left_stack:
                left_stack.pop()
            A = left_stack[-1] if left_stack else e1

        col = cols[cur_c]
        old_val = col[cur_r]
        delta = (a - old_val) % mod
        if delta:
            # dot(B, u)
            dotBu = 0
            val = 1
            for i in range(cur_r, K-1):
                dotBu += B[i] * val
                val = (val * col[i+1]) % mod
            dotBu += B[K-1] * val

            # dot(v, A)
            dotvA = 0
            val = 1
            for j in range(cur_r, 0, -1):
                dotvA += A[j] * val
                val = (val * col[j-1]) % mod
            dotvA += A[0] * val

            delta_ans = delta * (dotBu % mod) % mod * (dotvA % mod) % mod
            ans = (ans + delta_ans) % mod

        col[cur_r] = a
        out.append(str(ans))

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

Let's double-check the dotBu loop when K=1? Not possible, but if K=1, range(cur_r,0) etc. But K>=2. Fine.

Wait, in dotBu loop, if cur_r = K-1, range(K-1, K-1) empty, dotBu = B[K-1]*1. Correct. If cur_r < K-1, after loop, val = product col[r+1..K-1]? Let's trace K=3,r=0:
i=0: dot += B0*1; val = col1
i=1: dot += B1*col1; val = col1*col2
after loop dot += B2*col1*col2. u = [1, col1, col1*col2]. Correct.
r=1:
i=1: dot += B1*1; val=col2
after loop dot += B2*col2. u=[0,1,col2]. Correct.

dotvA loop K=3,r=2:
j=2: dot += A2*1; val=col1
j=1: dot += A1*col1; val=col0*col1
after loop dot += A0*col0*col1. v=[col0*col1, col1, 1]. Correct.
r=1:
j=1: dot += A1*1; val=col0
after loop dot += A0*col0. v=[col0,1,0]. Correct.

Now, one subtlety: In the update, `dotBu` uses `B` which is suffix after current column. But `B` is a vector of length K. The dot product with u should be `B^T u`. We computed. Good.

Now, let's think about the initial answer if `cur_c` is not the first column and left_stack contains aggregate vectors. We built left_stack by applying columns 0..cur_c-1. But if cur_c is large, left_stack[-1] is A. Good.

This includes the current column's cells. The initial vector e1 at column 0 boundary means X[0]=1, others 0. For column 1, Y[0]=a0*1 = A[1,1], Y[1]=a1*(Y0+0)=A[2,1]*A[1,1], etc. This matches F for first column. Good.

The suffix vector B = (T_L ... T_{c+1})^T eK. The answer = B^T (T_c A). Because eK^T (suffix * T_c * A) = (suffix^T eK)^T (T_c A). Yes.

Optimization ideas:
- If delta == 0, skip update loops. Many updates might set same value? Not guaranteed.
- Combine dotBu and dotvA? They are independent but both loops over K. Could compute them in one loop? dotBu goes upward from r, dotvA downward from r. We can compute both in a single loop over distance d = max(r, K-1-r)? Not straightforward.
- Use `while` loops? For loops are faster in Python than while generally.
- Use local variables for B, A, col, K, mod inside update. We already do.

In a 447x447 grid, the walk can bounce horizontally many times, so apply_col/transpose called often. So worst-case.

Can we reduce apply_col/transpose cost? Moving horizontally requires updating A or B. This is necessary. But maybe we can avoid moving stacks if the column doesn't change; in a square grid, a random walk changes column half the time. Still.

The update loops dotBu and dotvA are O(K). Could we compute dotBu and dotvA using the same column transformation? dotvA = Y[r-1]+A[r] where Y=T_c A. If we had Y, dotvA is O(1). But computing Y is O(K), same as dotvA loop. dotBu = B^T u, u is column suffix product. This could be precomputed for each row? The column values change, but we can maintain suffix products of each column? On update, suffix products for rows <=r change; updating them O(K). Not better.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353


def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    it = iter(data)

    H = int(next(it))
    W = int(next(it))

    grid = []
    for _ in range(H):
        row = [int(next(it)) % MOD for _ in range(W)]
        grid.append(row)

    Q = int(next(it))
    sh = int(next(it))
    sw = int(next(it))

    # Make the number of rows K = min(H, W).
    if H <= W:
        transposed = False
        K, L = H, W
        cols = [[grid[r][c] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]
        cur_r = sh - 1
        cur_c = sw - 1
    else:
        transposed = True
        K, L = W, H
        cols = [[grid[c][r] for r in range(K)] for c in range(L)]
        cur_r = sw - 1
        cur_c = sh - 1

    del grid

    def apply_col(c, X):
        a = cols[c]
        Y = [0] * K
        prev = 0
        for i in range(K):
            prev = (a[i] * (prev + X[i])) % MOD
            Y[i] = prev
        return Y

    def apply_col_transpose(c, X):
        a = cols[c]
        Y = [0] * K
        nxt = 0
        for i in range(K - 1, -1, -1):
            nxt = (a[i] * (X[i] + nxt)) % MOD
            Y[i] = nxt
        return Y

    e1 = [0] * K
    e1[0] = 1
    eK = [0] * K
    eK[K - 1] = 1

    # left_stack: aggregates of columns [0, cur_c)
    A = e1
    left_stack = []
    for c in range(cur_c):
        A = apply_col(c, A)
        left_stack.append(A)

    # right_stack: aggregates of columns (cur_c, L)
    B = eK
    right_stack = []
    for c in range(L - 1, cur_c, -1):
        B = apply_col_transpose(c, B)
        right_stack.append(B)

    # initial answer
    mid = apply_col(cur_c, A)
    ans = 0
    for i in range(K):
        ans += B[i] * mid[i]
    ans %= MOD

    out = []

    for _ in range(Q):
        d = next(it)
        a = int(next(it)) % MOD

        old_c = cur_c

        if not transposed:
            if d == b'U':
                cur_r -= 1
            elif d == b'D':
                cur_r += 1
            elif d == b'L':
                cur_c -= 1
            else:  # R
                cur_c += 1
        else:
            if d == b'U':
                cur_c -= 1
            elif d == b'D':
                cur_c += 1
            elif d == b'L':
                cur_r -= 1
            else:  # R
                cur_r += 1

        # Move the current column boundary.
        if cur_c > old_c:
            A = apply_col(old_c, A)
            left_stack.append(A)
            if right_stack:
                right_stack.pop()
            B = right_stack[-1] if right_stack else eK
        elif cur_c < old_c:
            B = apply_col_transpose(old_c, B)
            right_stack.append(B)
            if left_stack:
                left_stack.pop()
            A = left_stack[-1] if left_stack else e1

        # Update A[cur_r][cur_c].
        col = cols[cur_c]
        old_val = col[cur_r]
        delta = (a - old_val) % MOD

        if delta:
            # delta_T_c = u * v^T
            # dot(B, u)
            dotBu = 0
            val = 1
            for i in range(cur_r, K - 1):
                dotBu += B[i] * val
                val = (val * col[i + 1]) % MOD
            dotBu += B[K - 1] * val

            # dot(v, A)
            dotvA = 0
            val = 1
            for j in range(cur_r, 0, -1):
                dotvA += A[j] * val
                val = (val * col[j - 1]) % MOD
            dotvA += A[0] * val

            delta_ans = delta * (dotBu % MOD) % MOD * (dotvA % MOD) % MOD
            ans = (ans + delta_ans) % MOD

        col[cur_r] = a
        out.append(str(ans))

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
